Azimuthal instabilities on extremal Kerr
Dejan Gajic

Abstract
. We prove the existence of instabilities for the geometric linear wave equation on extremal Kerr spacetime backgrounds, which describe stationary black holes rotating at their maximally allowed angular velocity. These instabilities can be associated to non-axisymmetric azimuthal modes and are stronger than the axisymmetric instabilities discovered by Aretakis in [ Are15 ] . The existence of non-axisymmetric instabilities follows from a derivation of very precise stability properties of solutions: we determine therefore the precise, global, leading-order, late-time behaviour of solutions supported on a bounded set of azimuthal modes via energy estimates in both physical and frequency space. In particular, we obtain sharp, uniform decay-in-time estimates and we determine the coefficients and rates of inverse-polynomial late-time tails everywhere in the exterior of extremal Kerr black holes. We also demonstrate how non-axisymmetric instabilities leave an imprint in the radiation on future null infinity via the coefficients appearing in front of slowly decaying and oscillating late-time tails.



1.
Introduction

Extremal black holes are critical members of families of stationary black holes. They sit exactly at a phase transition from black hole to ‘‘naked singularity’’.
1
1

1

In this context, naked singularities are spacetimes that, from an evolutionary perspective, have geodesically incomplete future and past null infinities and arise from
singular
initial data. These are also called “super-extremal” spacetimes.
Criticality can be understood geometrically via the vanishing of the surface gravity associated to Killing vector fields that generate black hole event horizons.

In the context of the vacuum Einstein equations,

\textnormal{Ric}[g]=0,

(1.1)

such black holes are called
extremal Kerr black holes

[
Ker63
]
and criticality can be interpreted as the black holes rotating at their maximally allowed speed.

In this paper, we perform a mathematically rigorous investigation of the dynamical properties of extremal black holes in the context of the geometric, linear wave equation:

\square_{g_{M,a}}\phi=0,

(1.2)

on extremal Kerr spacetime backgrounds
(\mathcal{M},g_{M,a})
with mass
M
and specific angular momentum

|a|=M
. A mathematical study of (
1.2
) constitutes a first step towards addressing the dynamics of black holes that arise in the evolution of small perturbations of extremal Kerr initial data for (
1.1
).

Even the most elementary dynamical question, the question of
stability
, still remains widely open for small perturbations of extremal Kerr initial data. This may be contrasted with the problem of stability of
sub
-extremal Kerr, corresponding to
|a|<M
and describing black holes that rotate at speeds strictly below the maximal speed limit, for which tremendous progress has recently been achieved; see the discussion and references in §
1.2
.

Already at the level of the linear problem (
1.2
), the criticality of extremal Kerr leads to a significant departure from the sub-extremal case. In
[
Are15
]
, Aretakis showed that extremal Kerr black holes feature an
axisymmetric, asymptotic instability
along their future event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
: higher-order transversal derivatives of axisymmetric
\phi
generically blow up polynomially as time

\tau\to\infty
. The same type of instability is also present for linear wave equations on the simpler, non-rotating but electromagnetically charged (“extremal Reissner–Nordström”) black holes
[
Are11a
,
Are11b
]
.

An asymptotic instability at the
linear
level may lead to a genuine instability at the
nonlinear level
, i.e. the spacetimes arising from small perturbations of Kerr data could deviate significantly from extremal Kerr at late times. An asymptotic instability is, however, also compatible with
stability
at the nonlinear level. That is to say, while derivatives of the metric might blow up as
\tau\to\infty
, the metric itself could stay close to the unperturbed metric. Indeed, in the context of extremal Reissner–Nordström black holes, an analysis of a nonlinear analogue of (
1.2
) with a similar nonlinear structure to (
1.1
) has shown that the asymptotic instability remains asymptotic in the nonlinear setting and the solutions decay to zero
[
AAG20b
]
. A similar result is expected to hold in the setting of
axisymmetric
solutions to analogous nonlinear wave equations on extremal Kerr.

In this paper,
we go
beyond axisymmetry
and derive the existence of
stronger
asymptotic instabilities for non-axisymmetric solutions to (
1.2
)
. These
azimuthal instabilities
are derived via a sharp analysis of the
stability
properties of
\phi
in the form of the precise leading-order late-time behaviour (“late-time tails”) of azimuthal mode solutions
\phi_{m}
to (
1.2
) along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, with
m
an azimuthal number.
2
2

2

These are solutions with an azimuthal angular dependence
e^{im\varphi}
, where
m\in\mathbb{Z}
.
These tails feature
slower decay in time
when compared to the axisymmetric or sub-extremal settings.

Away from the event horizon and along future null infinity
\mathcal{I}^{+}
, we moreover derive the existence of slowly-decaying, oscillating late-time tails, which decay like
u^{-1}
and oscillate to leading-order with a frequency
m\upomega_{+}
,
where
\upomega_{+}
is the angular velocity of the black hole and
u
a standard Bondi time coordinate. This late-time behaviour is markedly different from the faster-decaying, non-oscillating sub-extremal late-time tails (“Price’s law”), as derived in
[
Hin22
,
AAG23
]
.

While the nonlinear analysis of
[
AAG20b
]
suggests stability of extremal Kerr within the restricted class of axisymmetric perturbations
3
3

3

Since the initial data in this setting is necessarily geodesically incomplete and extremal Kerr data has no trapped surfaces, “stability” in this context would correspond to one of the following scenarios: the convergence of the perturbed metric in a suitable choice of coordinates to the black hole exterior of 1) a nearby sub-extremal Kerr spacetime, 2) a nearby extremal Kerr spacetime, with an asymptotic instability at the level of metric derivatives along the event horizon (“horizon hair”), or 3) the failure to collapse to a black hole: the perturbed spacetime is close to the causal past of a finite subset of
\mathcal{I}^{+}
in extremal Kerr. See also
[
DHRT21
, §IV.2]
for a related discussion on the formulations of nonlinear stability of extremal Reissner–Nordström.
, the slow decay and the stronger nature of the instabilities derived in the present paper goes beyond the methods of
[
AAG20b
]
and tantalizes the possibility of a
departure from the Kerr family
in the end-point of the nonlinear evolution of small perturbations of extremal Kerr initial data.

The quantitative analysis of late-time tails along
\mathcal{I}^{+}
in the present paper also has observational implications, as it also implies the existence of characteristic slowly decaying, oscillating late-time tails at
late, but finite
time intervals in sub-extremal Kerr black holes that are sufficiently close to extremality (“near-extremal Kerr black holes”). One may think of such
intermediate late time tails
as carrying an imprint of the azimuthal instability that would be occurring at the event horizon of an extremal black hole.

1.1.
Main theorems

We now give the first, rough formulations of the main two theorems that are proved in the present paper. More precise versions are stated in §
4
, after an introduction of the relevant geometric notation in §§
2
–
3
.

Theorem 1.1

(The existence of azimuthal instabilities on extremal Kerr; rough version)
.

Non-axisymmetric solutions to (
1.2
), arising from suitably regular and decaying initial data feature at least one of the following asymptotic instabilities: generically,
4
4

4

The initial data for which
none
of these instabilities occur can be thought of as having infinite codimension.

(a)

first-order transversal derivatives of
\phi
grow at least like
\tau^{\frac{1}{2}}
in time along a sequence of times
\tau_{n}\to\infty
along the event horizon
and the
H^{1}_{\rm space}
-norm (the non-degenerate energy) is non-decaying in time, or

(b)

\phi\notin L^{2}_{\rm time}L^{2}_{\rm space}
, or

(c)

\partial\phi\notin L^{2}_{\rm time}L^{2}_{\rm space;loc}
away from the event horizon.

We refer to the instability
(a)
as an
azimuthal instability
as it originates from azimuthal modes
\phi_{m}
with
m\neq 0
, which are solutions with an azimuthal angular dependence
e^{im\varphi}
. In sub-extremal Kerr, or for axisymmetric solutions on extremal Kerr, all the derivatives
\partial\phi
can be uniformly bounded in time and the spacetime integrals of
|\phi|^{2}
and
|\partial\phi|^{2}
away from the horizon can be bounded via integrated energy (Morawetz) estimates. Hence, none of the instabilities
(a)
,
(b)
or
(c)
occur in these settings.

The key ingredient for proving Theorem
1.1
is the following precise analysis of the late-time behaviour of azimuthal modes
\phi_{m}
:

Theorem 1.2

(Late-time tails and stability for azimuthal modes on extremal Kerr; rough version)
.

Let
|m|\geq 2
. Under a
qualitative
global integrability assumption for
\phi_{m}
, the
H^{1}_{\rm space}
-norm of
\phi_{m}
is bounded and
r\phi_{m}
decays
quantitatively
in time
\tau
with the sharp rate
\tau^{-\frac{1}{2}}
along the future event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and
\tau^{-1}
away from the event horizon.

More precisely, with respect to coordinates
(v,\theta,\widetilde{\varphi})
along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
that are adapted to the Killing vector field generating the null hypersurface
\mathcal{H}^{+}
:

\phi_{m}|_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}(v,\theta,\widetilde{\varphi})\sim v^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{\ell\>:\>\alpha_{m\ell}<0}\mathfrak{h}_{m\ell}\left(a_{m\ell}\cdot e^{-i\left(\sqrt{-\alpha_{m\ell}}+m\right)\log v}+b_{m\ell}\cdot e^{i\left(\sqrt{-\alpha_{m\ell}}-m\right)\log v}\right)e^{im\widetilde{\varphi}}S_{m\ell}(\theta).

With respect to Bondi coordinates
(u,\theta,\varphi)
along future null infinity,

r\phi_{m}|_{\mathcal{I}^{+}}(u,\theta,\varphi)\sim u^{-1}e^{-im\upomega_{+}u}\sum_{\ell\>:\>\alpha_{m\ell}<0}\mathfrak{h}_{m\ell}\left(c_{m\ell}\cdot e^{-i\sqrt{-\alpha_{m\ell}}\log u}+d_{m\ell}\cdot e^{i\sqrt{-\alpha_{m\ell}}\log u}\right)e^{im\varphi}S_{m\ell}(\theta),

where

•

\alpha_{m\ell}=\Lambda_{+,m\ell}-2m^{2}+\frac{1}{4}
,

•

\Lambda_{+,m\ell}
is the eigenvalue of a natural angular operator defined on the 2-sphere
\mathcal{H}^{+}\cap\{v=0\}
, with eigenfunctions
e^{im\widetilde{\varphi}}S_{m\ell}(\theta)
,

•

\upomega_{+}
is the angular velocity of the black hole,

•

\mathfrak{h}_{m\ell}
is an integral of the initial data, projected to
e^{im\widetilde{\varphi}}S_{m\ell}(\theta)
, which is generically non-zero,

•

a_{m\ell},b_{m\ell},c_{m\ell},d_{m\ell}\in\mathbb{C}
are constants that are independent of the initial data.

We now give several remarks regarding Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
.

Remark 1.1
.

The instabilities
(b)
and
(c)
in Theorem
1.1
can be excluded with the assumption of appropriate, global, qualitative integrability assumptions for
\phi
, as in the statement of Theorem
1.2
. These assumptions can be be verified if one extends the analysis in the present paper from exactly extremal Kerr backgrounds to near-extremal Kerr backgrounds; see also §
1.4
. This will be explored in future work.

Remark 1.2
.

By combining the azimuthal instability
(a)
from Theorem
1.1
with the decay results in Theorem
1.2
, we can infer that the energy of azimuthal modes
\phi_{m}
with
|m|\geq 2
(as measured by
H^{1}
-norms along a foliation of spacelike constant-
\tau
hypersurfaces)
concentrates at the horizon
as
\tau\to\infty
, since it is non-decaying by Theorem
1.1
, but decays when restricted away from the horizon by Theorem
1.2
.

Remark 1.3
.

The azimuthal instability
(a)
differs from the axisymmetric instability following from
[
Are15
,
Are12
,
Are13b
]
(see Theorem
A
in §
1.2
) and the analogous extremal Reissner–Nordström instabilities from
[
Are11a
,
Are11b
]
in the following ways:

1)

Axisymmetric solutions have bounded, but non-decaying first-order derivatives along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, as shown in
[
Are12
]
and, in view of the related analysis in extremal Reissner–Nordström in
[
AAG20a
]
, non-degenerate energies, or
H^{1}_{\rm space}
norms, are expected to decay in time.

2)

The axisymmetric instability mechanism relies on the existence of axisymmetric conservation laws along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. In that case, the coefficient
H_{0}
appearing in front of the terms that blow up along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
is the value of the corresponding conserved charge (the “horizon hair”) and is therefore defined at the horizon. These conserved charges along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
form a close analogue of the Newman–Penrose constants
[
NP68
]
along
\mathcal{I}^{+}
. In contrast, the azimuthal instability in Theorem
1.1
does not follow from conservation laws, but instead follows from the precise knowledge of the late-time tails of azimuthal modes along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
.

3)

In the non-axisymmetric setting, the role of
H_{0}
is taken on by the constants
\mathfrak{h}_{m\ell}
appearing in Theorem
1.2
, which are not defined at the horizon but are integrals along the entire initial data hypersurface. As a result, the azimuthal instability does
not
become weaker by simply considering initial data supported away from
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, as is the case in axisymmetry; see
[
Are13b
]
. Instead, it becomes weaker for data which has vanishing constants
\mathfrak{h}_{m\ell}=0
.

Remark 1.4
.

The constants
\mathfrak{h}_{m\ell}
, which are responsible for the azimuthal instability (see Remark
1.3
), can be observed in the late-time behaviour of radiation fields
r\phi_{m}|_{\mathcal{I}^{+}}
, which also carries information about the rotation of the black hole via the oscillating factor
\frac{e^{-im\upomega_{+}u}(1+\log u\,O(u^{-1}))}{u}
. Since this late-time behaviour differs significantly from the more rapid, non-oscillating inverse polynomial decay in the sub-extremal setting
[
Hin22
,
AAG23
]
, it provides an
observational signature
of extremality and the existence of azimuthal instabilities.

For sub-extremal black holes that are close to extremality, the azimuthal instability will instead become a “transient instability” by Cauchy stability, i.e. quantities that blow up in extremal Kerr will instead become very large, but they will eventually decay asymptotically in time. This transient instability will leave an
imprint
along
\mathcal{I}^{+}
in the form of a transient, oscillating late-time tail at late, but finite time intervals.

This observational signature may be compared with the setting of extremal Reissner–Nordström, where (near)-extremality
cannot
immediately be inferred from the late-time decay rate along a single far-away causal curve, but requires additionally the consideration of integrals along the spheres foliating
\mathcal{I}^{+}

[
AAG18a
]
. See also
[
BKS19
,
BKS21
]
for additional numerical analysis on the observational signature in this setting.

1.2.
Previous work

In this section, we give an overview of previous works relevant to Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
.

Extremal black holes

A mathematical study of the dynamics of axisymmetric solutions to (
1.2
) was pioneered in the works of Aretakis
[
Are12
,
Are15
,
Are13b
]
, where the following theorem was proved:

Theorem A

(Axisymmetric instability on extremal Kerr;
[
Are12
,
Are15
,
Are13b
]
)
.

Consider suitably regular and decaying initial data for (
1.2
). Then

(i)

Generically, second-order derivatives of
\phi
grow at least with the rate
\tau
as
\tau\to\infty
along the future event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
.

(ii)

If the initial data are supported away from the future event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, then the growth in
(i)
occurs instead at the level of third-order derivatives.

(iii)

Axisymmetric solutions
\phi_{0}
decay at least as fast as
\tau^{-\frac{1}{2}}
along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and future null infinity
\mathcal{I}^{+}
, and at least as fast as
\tau^{-1}
in bounded regions of space away from
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, and their first-order derivatives are bounded in time.

Theorem
A
demonstrates the existence of
axisymmetric instabilities
for higher-order transversal derivatives along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. The underlying mechanism for instabilities is based on the existence of conservation laws along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, together with the assumption of a
qualitative
decay statement for
\phi
and its tangential derivatives along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. Under the global assumption of qualitative decay for
\phi
, the axisymmetric instabilities have been shown to hold in more general settings
[
LMRT13
,
Mur13
]
. See Remark
1.3
for a comparison of Theorem
A
with Theorem
1.1
.

In view of the existence of a bijection between the behaviour of
\phi_{m=0}
along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and
r\phi_{m=0}
in
\mathcal{I}^{+}
(valid only in axisymmetry)
[
CT84
,
BF13
]
, the conservation law of Aretakis along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
may be interpreted as a manifestation of the Newman–Penrose conservation law along
\mathcal{I}^{+}
that is present in general asymptotically flat settings
[
NP68
]
. The relation between conservation laws and characteristic initial data gluing problems for wave equations was investigated further in
[
Are17
]
and has recently been analyzed in the context of the Einstein equations in
[
ACR21a
,
ACR21c
,
ACR21b
,
CR22
]
.

The first analysis of non-axisymmetric solutions to (
1.2
) was performed by Teixeira da Costa in
[
TdC20
]
, where it was shown that there are no exponentially growing mode solutions on extremal Kerr, i.e. there is mode stability on extremal Kerr.

A related problem is the study of the geometric wave equation on extremal Reissner–Nordström black holes, which are charged but non-rotating analogues of extremal Kerr black holes. Due to the lack of rotation, the analysis on these spacetimes is greatly simplified and it mirrors the analysis of axisymmetric solutions to (
1.2
). In particular, these spacetimes also feature an instability mechanism based on conservation laws, and it was shown in
[
Are11a
,
Are11b
]
that analogues of (i) and (ii) from Theorem
A
also hold in this setting. Via a gluing analysis along the event horizon, spherically symmetric black hole spacetimes have also been constructed that are isometric to extremal Reissner–Nordström in the black hole exterior for sufficiently late times, but arise from regular, geodesically complete initial data
[
KU22
]
.

In the setting of extremal Reissner–Nordström, precise late-time decay properties and the existence of late-time tails were obtained in
[
AAG20a
]
:

Theorem B

(Late-time tails on extremal Reissner–Nordström;
[
AAG20a
]
)
.

Consider suitably regular and decaying initial data for the geometric wave equation on extremal Reissner–Nordström spacetimes. Then the corresponding solutions
\phi
display the following late-time behaviour:

\displaystyle\phi|_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}(v,\theta,\varphi)\sim

\displaystyle\>H_{0}v^{-1},

\displaystyle r\phi|_{\mathcal{I}^{+}}(u,\theta,\varphi)\sim

\displaystyle\>(H_{0}+Q)u^{-2},

with
H_{0}
the value of the conserved quantity at
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and
Q
proportional to the time-inverted Newman–Penrose constant.

Theorem
B
demonstrates that despite the presence of asymptotic instabilities along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, precise late-time decay results can be obtained. In particular, the late-time tails along
\mathcal{I}^{+}
can be split into two parts: a part related to the conservation law and instability along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, which comes with a constant
H_{0}
, and a part which is also present in the sub-extremal setting and comes with a constant
Q
that may be interpreted as the Newman–Penrose conserved quantity at
\mathcal{I}^{+}
associated to the
integral in time
of the spherically symmetric part of
\phi
. Unlike
H_{0}
, the constant
Q
can be expressed as an integral along the entire initial data hypersurface.

Due to the strong similarity of the analysis of axisymmetric solutions to (
1.2
) with general solutions to the geometric wave equation on extremal Reissner–Nordström, it is expected that the results of Theorem
B
can be extended to axisymmetric solutions to (
1.2
). The rates appearing in Theorem
B
may be contrasted with the more intricate and slower decay in Theorem
1.2
and the absence of the time-inverted Newman–Penrose constant
Q
in the leading-order asymptotics.

Recently, the analogue of the instability in (i) of Theorem
A
was also shown to hold in the context of the linearized Einstein–Maxwell equations on extremal Reissner–Nördstrom
[
Ape22
]
. In the nonlinear setting, the techniques developed in the proof of Theorem
B
have been applied to show that the instability remains asymptotic in the context of a nonlinear problem that models the nonlinear structure present in the Einstein equations
[
AAG20b
]
.
5
5

5

The asymptotic instability can also lead to a finite-in-time instability for nonlinear equations that have a different nonlinear structure; see
[
Are13a
]
.

The implications of the behaviour in Theorem
B
to the singularity properties in the black hole interior of extremal Reissner–Nordström and axisymmetric solutions to (
1.2
) was studied in
[
Gaj17a
,
Gaj17b
,
GL19
]
. In particular, it was shown that the Cauchy horizon in this setting is
more stable
than in the sub-extremal setting and the regularity of solutions at the Cauchy horizon may depend rather delicately on the leading and next-to-leading order late-time tails along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
.

Sub-extremal black holes

The mathematical analysis of the geometric wave equation on sub-extremal black holes has a rich history. Uniform boundedness and decay estimates for solutions to the wave equation on the full sub-extremal range of Kerr parameters were obtained by Dafermos–Rodnianski–Shlapentokh-Rothman in
[
DRSR16
]
; see also the references therein for a comprehensive list of intermediate and related results.

In
[
DRSR16
]
, the following key mechanisms were exploited to establish weak, integrated energy estimates or
Morawetz estimates
in sub-extremal Kerr: 1) the red-shift effect (first discussed as a decay mechanism in
[
DR09
]
), 2) the decoupling of
null geodesic trapping
and
superradiance
in Fourier space, and 3) quantitative mode stability, first established qualitatively in
[
Whi89
]
and then quantitatively in
[
SR15
]
.

Passing from integrated energy energy estimates to inverse-polynomial energy decay in time involves an application of the
r
-weighted estimates of Dafermos–Rodnianski
[
DR10
]
, which are not restricted to Kerr spacetimes, but hold on general asymptotically flat spacetimes, as shown in
[
Mos16
]
. The analysis in
[
DRSR16
]
has recently also been extended to the setting of the Teukolsky equations of linearized gravity
[
SRTdC20
,
SRTdC23
]
.

The step from uniform upper bound decay estimates to precise leading-order decay estimates and late-time tails (analogous to Theorems
1.2
and
B
) was first made in
[
AAG18c
,
AAG18b
]
in the context of spherically symmetric, sub-extremal black hole spacetimes and then extended to sub-extremal Kerr in
[
AAG23
]
, where it was moreover explored how the late-time tails corresponding to different angular modes interact; see also
[
Hin22
]
for a different approach that involves precise resolvent estimates near the zero time-frequency.

A useful subset of the sub-extremal Kerr family to consider first are the slowly rotating Kerr spacetimes, which have a rotation parameter
a
that satisfies
|a|\ll M
. These include the Schwarzschild spacetimes for which
a=0
. Due to the smallness of the parameter
a
, the issue of superradiance and mode stability, which are absent when
a=0
, are easier to handle. Significant advances have been made in this setting regarding decay and stability at the level of the nonlinear Einstein equations. The nonlinear stability of Schwarzschild was proved for polarized axisymmetric initial data in
[
KS20
]
. Data without symmetry were considered in
[
DHRT21
]
and the full, codimension 3 subset of initial data perturbations were identified for which stability holds. More recently, a proof of nonlinear stability of slowly rotating Kerr without symmetry restrictions was completed in a series of works
[
KS21
,
KS22a
,
GKS22
,
KS22b
,
She22
]
.

Inverse-square potentials

A setting that roughly models the situation “between” sub-extremal Kerr and extremal Kerr are wave equations with negative inverse-square potentials on Schwarzschild spacetimes. Here, future null infinity
\mathcal{I}^{+}
plays the role of the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
in extremal Kerr. The absence of conservation laws for non-axisymmetric solutions along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
in extremal Kerr is reflected in the breakdown of the Newman–Penrose conservation law at
\mathcal{I}^{+}
. The proofs for deriving the precise late-time behaviour of solutions to the wave equation without potential (see
[
AAG23
]
and the earlier works
[
AAG18c
,
AAG18b
]
) rely crucially on the existence of this conservation law, and they therefore break down. In
[
Gaj22
]
, a new, more robust, method was developed for dealing with the absence of conservation laws and deriving late-time tails which depend on the coefficient in the leading-order term of the inverse square potential. See also upcoming work on establishing these tails in a different way for wave equations with inverse-square potentials and electromagnetically charged wave equations on Minkowski
[
GVdM23
]
and see
[
BGRM22
]
for recent work on late-time tails for wave equations with inverse-square potentials on Minkowski.

Numerics and heuristics

The mathematical analysis described in the above paragraphs was preceded by a large number of works with numerical and heuristic analysis in the physics literature. In particular, the pointwise blow-up of transversal derivations in the azimuthal instability of Theorem
1.1
was predicted from a heuristic analysis of the Teukolsky equation in frequency space near the frequency
\omega=m\upomega_{+}
by Casals–Gralla–Zimmerman in
[
CGZ16
]
, who moreover predicted the decay rates of late-time tails along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
consistent with Theorem
1.2
. Growth along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
with consistent rates was also observed numerically in
[
HBB13
]
. See also the related heuristics in
[
HR17
]
on instabilities in the near-horizon spacetimes corresponding to extremal Kerr.

The late-time behaviour in Theorem
1.2
was first predicted (in bounded regions in space, away from
\mathcal{H}^{+}
) by Andersson–Glampedakis
[
AG00
]
via a heuristic analysis of slowly decaying
quasinormal modes
(“zero-damped modes”; see for example
[
Hod08
]
for a discussion) on near-extremal Kerr black holes with complex time-frequencies that are expected to accumulate to the real frequency
\omega=m\upomega_{+}
. While each quasinormal mode decays exponentially in time, their decay rate goes to zero in the extremal limit. The analysis in
[
AG00
]
suggested that the
sum
over these modes should give rise to an inverse-polynomial tail with decay rate
t^{-1}
and an overall oscillating factor
e^{-im\upomega_{+}t}
.

The analysis of
[
AG00
]
was later revisited, heuristically and numerically, in
[
YZZ+13
]
. The numerics in
[
YZZ+13
]
predicted moreover that the slowly-decaying late-time tail could constitute a dominant part of
\phi
at relatively
early
times, for initial data that are relevant when modelling black hole mergers. This stands in contrast with the expectation that the late-time tails in sub-extremal Kerr become dominant only at relatively
late
times. This strengthens further the case for slowly decaying late-time tails in extremal Kerr serving as a promising observational signature in the gravitational radiation emitted by astrophysical systems settling down to near-extremal Kerr black holes.

Note that
nonlinear
aspects of the dynamics of extremal Reissner–Nordström black holes were probed numerically in
[
MRT13
]
.

1.3.
The main difficulties compared to the axisymmetric and sub-extremal settings

In this section, we give an overview of the key difficulties enountered when proving Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
. We will revisit these difficulties and outline how they are resolved in the present paper in §
5
.

Integrated energy estimates: absence of (degenerate) red-shift

A key ingredient towards establishing energy decay estimate on sub-extremal black holes is the red-shift estimate, which, schematically, gives the following integrated energy estimate for solutions
\phi
to the wave equation:

\kappa\int_{0}^{T}\left[\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r_{+}\leq r\leq r_{+}+\epsilon\}}|\phi|^{2}+|\partial\phi|^{2}d\mu_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\right]\,d\tau\lesssim\int_{\Sigma_{0}\cap\{r_{+}\leq r\leq r_{+}+2\epsilon\}}|\phi|^{2}+|\partial\phi|^{2}d\mu_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\\
+\int_{0}^{T}\left[\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r_{+}+\epsilon\leq r\leq r_{+}+2\epsilon\}}|\phi|^{2}+|\partial\phi|^{2}d\mu_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\right]\,d\tau.

Here
r_{+}
is the radius of the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
,
\epsilon\ll r_{+}
and
d\mu_{\Sigma_{\tau}}
is the induced volume form on the spacelike hypersurface or time slice
\Sigma_{\tau}
. The constant
\kappa>0
denotes the surface gravity associated to the future event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, which appears as a proportionality constant in the following equation:

\nabla_{K}K=\kappa K,

where
K
is a Killing vector field which is a null generator of the event horizon. The above integrated estimate allows one to extend integrated energy estimates valid in
\{r>r_{+}+\epsilon\}
up to
\{r\geq r_{+}\}
.

In extremal Kerr,
\kappa=0
, so the red-shift estimate breaks down. Nevertheless, degenerate versions of the red-shift estimate remain valid for axisymmetric solutions to (
1.2
). For example,

\kappa\int_{0}^{T}\left[\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r_{+}\leq r\leq r_{+}+\epsilon\}}|\phi|^{2}+(r-r_{+})|\partial\phi|^{2}d\mu_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\right]\,d\tau\lesssim\int_{\Sigma_{0}\cap\{r_{+}\leq r\leq r_{+}+2\epsilon\}}|\phi|^{2}+|\partial\phi|^{2}d\mu_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\\
+\int_{0}^{T}\left[\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r_{+}+\epsilon\leq r\leq r_{+}+2\epsilon\}}|\phi|^{2}+|\partial\phi|^{2}d\mu_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\right]\,d\tau.

For non-axisymmetric solutions,
even the degenerate versions of the red-shift estimate break down
. Furthermore, while in the case of axisymmetric solutions
\phi_{0}
, the energy current corresponding to
K
is non-negative near
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, i.e.

\mathbb{T}[\phi_{0}](K,\mathbf{n}_{\Sigma_{\tau}})\geq 0,

with
\mathbb{T}
the energy momentum tensor and
\mathbf{n}_{\Sigma_{\tau}}
the normal to the spacelike hypersurfaces
\Sigma_{\tau}
, the non-negativity property breaks down for non-axisymmetric solutions, since
K
is not causal in any open neighbourhood of
\mathcal{H}^{+}
.

Integrated energy estimates: superradiance

Let
T
denote the Killing vector field that is timelike and normalized at
r=\infty
. In all rotating (
a\neq 0
) spacetimes,
T
fails to be causal in the
ergoregion

r<M\left(1+\sqrt{1+a^{2}\cos\theta}\right),

with
r
and
\theta
Boyer–Lindquist coordinates. The existence of an ergoregion leads to
superradiance
, which is an energy amplification effect that may be interpreted as “energy extracted from the black hole”. At the level of the Fourier transform of azimuthal modes
\phi_{m}
along integral curves of
T
, superradiance affects the following frequency range:

0<m\omega<m^{2}\upomega_{+}.

Note in particular that for axisymmetric
\phi
,
m=0
, so the above frequency range is empty and superradiance is not present. For bounded
m
, we can divide solutions into those with
high
angular frequencies
\Lambda
and those with bounded
angular
frequencies
\Lambda\lesssim m^{2}
(see §
6
for a precise definition of the angular frequency
\Lambda
). In both cases, the analysis from the sub-extremal setting
breaks down
and new techniques are required to obtain the desired estimates in Fourier space.

Instabilities: absence of conservation laws

The instability mechanism underlying the proof of (i) of Theorem
A
relies on the existence of conservation laws along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
for axisymmetric
\phi
in extremal Kerr. For this reason, asymptotic instabilities for transversal derivatives
\partial^{2}\phi_{0}
along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
follow immediately from the conservation of an appropriate linear combination of
\phi
and
\partial\phi
, together with a
qualitative
decay statement for
\phi
and its tangential derivatives along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
.

This stands in stark contrast with non-axisymmetric solutions, for which there is no conservation law available along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. Deriving an instability in this setting requires instead sharp,
quantitative
decay statements for
\phi
and its tangential derivatives along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. That is to say, one needs to derive the precise late-time tails for
\phi
before deriving the existence of instabilities.

Late-time tails and time integrals

Once weak, integrated estimates have been established for
\phi
, one needs a decay mechanism to derive sharp decay estimates and to derive the precise leading-order behaviour (late-time tails) of
\phi
. In the sub-extremal setting, such a mechanism was developed in
[
AAG18c
,
AAG18b
,
AAG23
]
and it revolved around
time integrals of
\phi
, which are integrals of
\phi
along the integral curves of the vector field
T
, together with appropriate hierarchies of
r
-weighted energy estimates far away from the black hole, first derived in
[
DR10
]
, and an exploitation of conservation laws along future null infinity
\mathcal{I}^{+}
.

In the case of axisymmetric
\phi
and, analogously, solutions to the wave equation on extremal Reissner–Nordström, a similar mechanism can be exploited, which depends also on the existence of hierarchies of degenerate energy estimates near
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and conservation laws along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
.

In view of the aforementioned breakdown of the degenerate red-shift estimates and the absence of conservation laws for non-axisymmetric
\phi
,
the sub-extremal decay mechanism breaks down
. Furthermore, whereas there is an unambiguous notion of “time integral” for axisymmetric
\phi
in extremal Kerr, associated to the vector field
T
, which agrees with
K
when acting on axisymmetric
\phi
, there are multiple possible notions of time integrals in the non-axisymmetric, extremal setting.

1.4.
Remaining questions

We list here some remaining questions and problems, which are not addressed in the present paper, but will be explored in future investigations.

•

Uniform frequency-space analysis on near-extremal Kerr.
Extending the frequency-space analysis in the present paper to
|a|\leq M
would allow one to prove the qualitative integrability assumptions of Theorem
1.2
and to rule out instabilities
(b)
and
(c)
in Theorem
1.1
. While most of the frequency-space analysis in the present paper includes near-extremal spacetimes, it remains to revisit the estimates in the bounded, frequency regime, with
|\omega-m\upomega_{+}|\ll 1
and extend them to the
|a|\leq M
setting.

•

Uniform estimates in
m
.
The analysis in the present paper is concerned with fixed azimuthal modes, or bounded
m
. This means that we do not keep careful track of the
m
-dependence in all the estimates. Extending the results of the present paper uniformly in
m
would require revisiting the analysis in the frequency regime
|\omega-m\upomega_{+}|\ll|m|
, which is then no longer a bounded frequency regime. In view of the existence of trapped null geodesics arbitrarily close to
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, one would expect any extension of the integrated energy estimates to unbounded
m
to come with an additional loss of azimuthal
\partial_{\varphi}
derivatives. As this frequency regime is both superradiant and is affected by trapping, it could also lead to more severe deviations from the bounded-
m
setting and perhaps even to stronger instabilities that are not visible for bounded
m
.

•

Nonlinear analysis.
In order to address the effects on nonlinearities in the Einstein equations to the presence of azimuthal instabilities in the linearized setting, one could consider model nonlinear wave equations of the form:

\square_{g}\phi=A(\phi)\cdot g^{-1}(d\phi,d\phi),

which satisfy the
null condition
and are therefore consistent with the nonlinear structure present in the Einstein equations. Even when considering initial data supported on a fixed azimuthal mode
\phi_{m}
, all the other modes are excited due to the presence of the nonlinearity and one needs to first understand uniformly what happens to the estimates in the present paper when
m\to\infty
.

The additional difficulties faced when considering
m\to\infty
notwithstanding, we can make the optimistic supposition for a moment that the growth and decay estimates in Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
remain valid uniformly in
m
.

Since the nonlinearity is of the form
g^{-1}(d\phi,d\phi)
, growing transversal derivatives along the event horizon along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
are not immediately fatal as will be multiplied by decaying tangential derivatives, so it is
in principle
possible to obtain global existence and uniqueness of solutions and show that the behaviour of the linear problem persists. Indeed, the above null structure of the nonlinearity was used in
[
AAG20b
]
to show that the instability in extremal Reissner–Nordström remains asymptotic, just like in the linear problem.

Given the slower decay and stronger azimuthal instabilities in the present paper, the methods of
[
AAG20b
]
break down and new methods need to be developed to perform an analysis of nonlinear wave equations satisfying the null condition. The expected loss of derivatives in integrated energy estimates when considering the limit
m\to\infty
and the resulting loss of derivatives in (degenerate) energy boundedness estimates would form additional difficulties for the nonlinear problem.

1.5.
Outline of the remainder of the paper

We provide here an outline of the structure of the remainder of the paper.

•

In §
2
and §
3
we introduce the necessary geometric preliminaries and basic facts regarding linear wave equations on Kerr spacetimes, as well as the main notation in the rest of the paper. We moreover derive some properties of the coefficients
\alpha_{\ell m}
from Theorem
1.2
and discuss stationary, or
K
-invariant solutions to the wave equation on extremal Kerr.

•

Then, we state precise versions of Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
in §
4
and sketch how they are proved in §
5
.

•

The frequency-space analysis of the paper is fully contained in §
6
, resulting in integrated energy estimates in physical space, which are derived in §
7
.

•

The key hierarchies of
r
-weighted (in far-away regions) and
(r-r_{+})
-weighted (in near-horizon regions) energy estimates are derived in §
8
. Together with the integrated energy estimates in §
7
, these form the key ingredient for deriving energy decay estimates for
K
-derivatives in §
9
.

•

In §
10
, we construct time integrals of
\phi_{m}
along integral curves of the vector field
K
and we derive the necessary elliptic-type estimates. Then we combine these with the energy decay estimates from §
9
in §
11
to obtain decay (energy and pointwise) estimates for
\phi_{m}
.

•

Finally, we use the precise energy and pointwise decay estimates from §
11
to derive azimuthal instabilities in §
12
.

1.6.
Acknowledgements

The author would like to thank Rita Teixeira da Costa and Yakov Shlapentokh-Rothman for useful discussions.

2.
Preliminaries: geometry and foliations

In this section, we introduce the geometric properties that are required for the analysis in the paper.

2.1.
The spacetime metric

In this section, we introduce the 2-parameter family of
Kerr spacetimes

(\mathcal{M}_{M,a},g_{M,a})
and the corresponding geometric notation.

Let
M>0
and
a\in\mathbb{R}
with
|a|\leq M
. Then
\mathcal{M}_{M,a}
is the following manifold-with-boundary:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{M,a}=

\displaystyle\>\mathbb{R}\times[r_{+},\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{2},

\displaystyle r_{+}=

\displaystyle\>M+\sqrt{M^{2}-a^{2}}.

The constant
M
is the
mass
of the Kerr black hole and
a\cdot M
can be considered its
specific angular momentum
, or its angular momentum/mass.

We can cover
\mathcal{M}_{M,a}
with coordinates
(v,r,\theta,\varphi_{*})
, where
v\in\mathbb{R}
,
r\in[r_{+},\infty)
and
(\theta,\varphi_{*})
denote standard spherical coordinates on the unit round 2-sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2}
(which degenerate at a half circle connecting the north and south pole). In these coordinates, the Lorentzian metric
g_{M,a}
takes the following form:

g_{M,a}=-\rho^{-2}(\Delta-a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta)dv^{2}+2dvdr-4Mar\rho^{-2}\sin^{2}\theta dvd\varphi_{*}-2a\sin^{2}\theta drd\varphi_{*}\\
+\rho^{2}d\theta^{2}+\rho^{-2}((r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}-a^{2}\Delta\sin^{2}\theta)\sin^{2}\theta d\varphi_{*}^{2},

(2.1)

with

\displaystyle\rho^{2}=

\displaystyle\>r^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta,

\displaystyle\Delta=

\displaystyle\>r^{2}-2Mr+a^{2}=(r-r_{+})(r-r_{-}),

\displaystyle r_{-}=

\displaystyle\>M-\sqrt{M^{2}-a^{2}}.

The above local expression for
g_{M,a}
is called the
ingoing Eddington–Finkelstein
form of the Kerr metric.

The 1-parameter subfamily
g_{|a|,a}
of Kerr spacetimes for which
M=|a|
are known as the
extremal Kerr spacetimes
.

The inverse metric
g^{-1}_{M,a}
corresponding to
g_{M,a}
then takes the following form:

g^{-1}_{M,a}=a^{2}\rho^{-2}\sin\theta^{2}\theta\partial_{v}\otimes\partial_{v}+\rho^{-2}(r^{2}+a^{2})[\partial_{v}\otimes\partial_{r}+\partial_{r}\otimes\partial_{v}]+\rho^{-2}\Delta\partial_{r}\otimes\partial_{r}\\
+a\rho^{-2}[(\partial_{v}+\partial_{r})\otimes\partial_{\varphi_{*}}+\partial_{\varphi_{*}}\otimes(\partial_{v}+\partial_{r})]+\rho^{-2}[\partial_{\theta}\otimes\partial_{\theta}+\sin^{-2}\theta\partial_{\varphi_{*}}\otimes\partial_{\varphi_{*}}].

(2.2)

We introduce the following notation for submanifolds of
\mathcal{M}_{M,a}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{+}:=

\displaystyle\>\{r=r_{+}\}\quad\textnormal{is called the (future) event horizon},

\displaystyle\mathring{\mathcal{M}_{M,a}}:=

\displaystyle\>\mathcal{M}_{M,a}\setminus\mathcal{H}^{+},

\displaystyle S^{2}_{v^{\prime},r^{\prime}}:=

\displaystyle\>\{r=r^{\prime}\}\cap\{v=v^{\prime}\}\>\>\textnormal{with $v^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}$ and $r^{\prime}\in[r_{+},\infty)$ are diffeomorphic to $\mathbb{S}^{2}$}.

We will moreover consider the following functions:

\displaystyle r_{*}=

\displaystyle\>\int_{3M}^{r}\frac{r^{\prime 2}+a^{2}}{\Delta}\,dr^{\prime}\quad\textnormal{on $\mathring{\mathcal{M}}_{M,a}$},

\displaystyle t=

\displaystyle\>v-r_{*}\quad\textnormal{on $\mathring{\mathcal{M}}_{M,a}$},

\displaystyle u=

\displaystyle\>v-2r_{*}=t-r_{*}\quad\textnormal{on $\mathring{\mathcal{M}}_{M,a}$},

\displaystyle\varphi=

\displaystyle\>\varphi_{*}+\int_{r}^{\infty}\frac{a}{\Delta}\,dr^{\prime}\>\>\textnormal{mod}\,2\pi\quad\textnormal{on $\mathring{\mathcal{M}}_{M,a}$},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\varphi}=

\displaystyle\>\varphi_{*}-\upomega_{+}v\>\>\textnormal{mod}\,2\pi\quad\textnormal{on $\mathcal{M}_{M,a}$},

where
\upomega_{+}=\frac{a}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}
can be interpreted as the “angular velocity of the Kerr black hole”, assigned by stationary observers at infinity. The coordinates
(v,r,\theta,\widetilde{\varphi})
may be interpreted as “rotating with the Kerr black hole”, because the integral curves of the null generator of
\mathcal{H}^{+}
correspond to the level sets
\{(v,r,\theta,\widetilde{\varphi})=(v,r_{+},\theta_{0},\widetilde{\varphi}_{0})\}
, with
(\theta_{0},\widetilde{\varphi}_{0})
constant.

The functions
(t,r,\theta,\varphi)
define coordinates on the manifold
\mathring{\mathcal{M}}_{M,a}
and are called
Boyer–Lindquist coordinates
.

To simplify the notation, we will frequently omit one parameter in the 2-parameter family of spacetimes by fixing
r_{+}=1
, which implies that:

\displaystyle r_{-}=

\displaystyle\>a^{2},

\displaystyle M=

\displaystyle\>\frac{1}{2}(1+a^{2}).

Then extremal Kerr spacetimes correspond simply to the choices
a=\pm 1
.

2.2.
Foliations and conformal coordinates

In this section, we introduce a foliation of
\mathcal{M}_{M,a}
by appropriate isometric spacelike hypersurfaces, i.e. a “time-slicing”.

Let
\mathbbm{h}:[r_{+},\infty)\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
be a smooth function, such that for all
r\in[r_{+},\infty)
:

\displaystyle\frac{2(r^{2}+a^{2})}{\Delta}-\mathbbm{h}(r)=

\displaystyle\>O_{\infty}(r^{-2}),

\displaystyle\mathbbm{h}(r)\Delta\left(\frac{2(r^{2}+a^{2})}{\Delta}-\mathbbm{h}(r)\right)>

\displaystyle\>a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta,

Then we define the
time function

\tau
on
\mathcal{M}_{M,a}
as follows:

\tau=v-\int_{r_{+}}^{r}\mathbbm{h}(r^{\prime})\,dr^{\prime}-v_{0},

with
v_{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Define

\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}}=\{\tau=\tau^{\prime}\}.

The analysis in the present paper, will be restricted to the spacetime subset:

\mathcal{R}:=\{\tau\geq 0\}.

We also denote

\Sigma:=\Sigma_{0}.

See Figure
1
below for a picture.

Figure 1
.
A 2-dimensional representation of the region under consideration in the spacetime
\mathcal{M}_{M,a}
, with the hypersurfaces
\Sigma_{\tau}
and the shaded region depicting
\mathcal{R}
. Each point in the picture represents a 2-sphere, composed of all points with
(v,r)
constant.

2.3.
Key vector fields

Kerr spacetimes are stationary and axisymmetric, which is reflected in the
v
- and
\varphi_{*}
-independence of the metric components in (
2.1
). The following vector fields are therefore Killing vector fields:

\displaystyle T:=

\displaystyle\>\partial_{v},

\displaystyle\Phi:=

\displaystyle\>\partial_{\varphi_{*}},

with respect to the coordinates
(v,r,\theta,\varphi_{*})
.

The linear combination:

K:=T+\upomega_{+}\Phi

plays a particularly important role, as it is a null generator of
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. Note that
K=\partial_{v}
in
(v,r,\theta,\widetilde{\varphi})
coordinates.

We moreover introduce:

\displaystyle Y=

\displaystyle\>\partial_{r},

\displaystyle\underline{L}=

\displaystyle-\frac{\Delta}{2(r^{2}+a^{2})}Y,

\displaystyle L=

\displaystyle\>T+\frac{a}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\Phi-\underline{L},

Note that
L(v)=-\underline{L}(u)=1
. It is straightforward to verify the following causality properties for
r_{+}=1
:

\displaystyle g(T,T)<

\displaystyle\>0\quad\textnormal{in}\quad\{\Delta-a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta>0\}\subset\{r<2M\},

\displaystyle g(L,L)=

\displaystyle\>g(\underline{L},\underline{L})=0,

\displaystyle g(K,K)=

\displaystyle\>-\frac{(2+a^{2}+a^{2}\cos(2\theta))^{2}}{4(1+a^{2})^{2}}(1-a^{2})(r-1)+\left(-1+\frac{a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta(6+2a^{2}-a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta)}{1+a^{2}}\right)(r-1)^{2}

\displaystyle+O((r-1)^{3})\quad\textnormal{when fixing $r_{+}=1$}.

Note therefore that
K
is null along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
(
r=1
) and, in sub-extremal Kerr spacetimes, it extends as a timelike vector field in a neighbourhood of the form
\{r-r_{+}<\delta_{a}\}
, with
\delta_{a}>0
suitably small (depending on
a
). In extremal Kerr spacetimes, on the other hand,
K
is not uniformly timelike or spacelike in a region of the form
\{r-r_{+}\leq\delta\}
, no matter how small we take
\delta>0
.

We denote further:

\displaystyle X:=

\displaystyle\>Y+\mathbbm{h}T,

\displaystyle\widetilde{X};=

\displaystyle\>Y+\mathbbm{h}K=X+\upomega_{+}\mathbbm{h}\Phi.

The vector fields
X
and
\widetilde{X}
are tangential to
\Sigma_{\tau}
. Note that
X=\partial_{r}
in
(\tau,r,\theta,\varphi_{*})
coordinates and
\widetilde{X}=\partial_{r}
in
(\tau,r,\theta,\widetilde{\varphi})
coordinates.

Finally, we denote

{\mathfrak{X}}_{*}=\frac{\Delta}{r^{2}+a^{2}}Y+T+\frac{a}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\Phi.

We can express
{\mathfrak{X}}_{*}=\partial_{r_{*}}
in
(t,r_{*},\theta,\varphi)
coordinates.

We moreover introduce the Carter operator

Q:=\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}-\Phi^{2}+\sin^{2}\theta T^{2}.

From standard elliptic estimates on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
we have the following inequality for
f\in H^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
:

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|f|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}f|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{2}f|^{2}\,d\sigma\leq C\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|Qf|^{2}+|\Phi^{2}f|^{2}+|K\Phi f|^{2}+|K^{2}f|^{2}\,d\sigma.

2.4.
Differential operators on
\mathbb{S}^{2}

We introduce the following second-order differential operator on the unit round 1-sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2}
:

\not{\mathcal{D}}(\cdot):=\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}(\cdot)+a^{2}\upomega_{+}^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\Phi^{2}(\cdot),

(2.3)

where
\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}
is the Laplacian on the unit 1-sphere.

The operator
\not{\mathcal{D}}
appears in the purely angular part of the wave operator
\square_{g_{M,a}}
. This follows by writing down
\square_{g}(\cdot)
in terms of the vector fields
(K,Y,\Phi)
; see (iv) of Lemma
3.1
.

We will now relate
\not{\mathcal{D}}
to the differential operator corresponding to
oblate spheroidal harmonics
.

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{R}
and consider the following operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
:

P(\nu)=-\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\theta}(\sin\theta\partial_{\theta}(\cdot))-\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\theta}\partial_{\varphi}^{2}(\cdot)-\nu^{2}\cos^{2}\theta(\cdot).

Note that
P(\nu)
is self-adjoint, so there exists an orthonormal basis of eigenfunctions. Since
[P(\nu),\partial_{\varphi}]=0
, we can decompose these eigenfunctions by employing eigenfunctions
\{e^{im\varphi}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
of
\partial_{\varphi}
:

S_{m\ell}(\theta;\nu)e^{im\varphi},

with
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}
labels, such that
-\ell\leq m\leq\ell
. We denote the corresponding eigenvalues of
P(\nu)
by
\lambda_{m\ell}^{(\nu)}
, with
\lambda_{m\ell}^{(\nu)}<\lambda_{m(\ell+1)}^{(\nu)}
, and note that they satisfy the inequalities:

\displaystyle\lambda_{m\ell}^{(\nu)}+\nu^{2}\geq

\displaystyle\>|m|(|m|+1),

(2.4)

\displaystyle\lambda_{m\ell}^{(\nu)}+\nu^{2}\geq

\displaystyle\>2|m\nu|.

(2.5)

We will moreover use the following shorthand notation: let
|a|\leq M
, then

\displaystyle P:=

\displaystyle\>P\left(a\upomega_{+}\right)=-\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\theta}(\sin\theta\partial_{\theta}(\cdot))-\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\theta}\partial_{\varphi}^{2}(\cdot)-a^{2}\upomega_{+}^{2}m^{2}\cos^{2}\theta(\cdot),

\displaystyle S_{m\ell}(\theta):=

\displaystyle\>S_{m\ell}\left(\theta;am\upomega_{+}\right),

\displaystyle\lambda_{m\ell}:=

\displaystyle\>\lambda_{m\ell}^{\left(a\upomega_{+}\right)}.

We can relate
P
as follows to
\not{\mathcal{D}}

-\not{\mathcal{D}}(\cdot)=P(\cdot)-a^{2}\upomega_{+}^{2}\Phi^{2},

so by (
2.4
), the following inequality holds:

-\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\Re(\not{\mathcal{D}}(S_{m\ell}e^{im\widetilde{\varphi}})\overline{S}_{m\ell}e^{-im\widetilde{\varphi}})\,d\sigma\geq|m|(|m|+1)\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|S_{m\ell}|^{2}\,d\sigma.

We moreover have that for all
\theta\in(0,\pi)

\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\theta}+a^{2}\upomega_{+}^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\geq 1+a^{2}\upomega_{+}^{2},

so we also have that

\begin{split}-\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\Re(\not{\mathcal{D}}S_{m\ell}e^{im\widetilde{\varphi}}\overline{S}_{m\ell}e^{-im\widetilde{\varphi}})\,d\sigma=&\>\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|\partial_{\theta}S_{m\ell}|^{2}+\left(\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\theta}+a^{2}\upomega_{+}^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\right)m^{2}|S_{m\ell}|^{2}\,d\sigma\\
\geq&\>\left(1+a^{2}\upomega_{+}^{2}\right)m^{2}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|S_{m\ell}|^{2}\,d\sigma.\end{split}

Denote
\Lambda_{+,m\ell}=\lambda_{m\ell}+a^{2}\upomega_{+}^{2}m^{2}
. Then

\not{\mathcal{D}}(e^{im\varphi}S_{m\ell}(\theta))=-\Lambda_{+,m\ell}e^{im\widetilde{\varphi}}S_{m\ell}(\theta).

and, by the above,

\Lambda_{+,m\ell}\geq\max\{(1+a^{2}\upomega_{+}^{2})|m|,|m|+1\}|m|.

(2.6)

In particular, for
r_{+}=1
and
|a|=1
,

\Lambda_{+,m\ell}\geq\max\left\{\frac{5}{4}|m|,|m|+1\right\}|m|.

Define

|\widetilde{\not{\nabla}}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}(\cdot)|^{2}:=|{\not{\nabla}}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}(\cdot)|^{2}+a^{2}\upomega_{+}^{2}\sin^{2}\theta|\Phi(\cdot)|^{2},

with
\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}
denoting the covariant derivative with respect to the metric on the unit 1-sphere.

Let
r_{+}=1
and
|a|=1
. Then the following quantity will play a key role in the paper:

\alpha_{m\ell}:=\Lambda_{+,m\ell}-2m^{2}+\frac{1}{4}.

The late-time asymptotics and sharp decay rates that we will derive follow from the fact that
\alpha_{m|m|}>0
for
|m|\geq 2
. This is proved in the lemma below.

Lemma 2.1
.

For
|m|=1
,
\alpha_{|m|m}\geq\frac{1}{4}
and for
|m|\geq 2
,
\alpha_{m|m|}<0
.

Proof.

The inequality for
|m|=1
follows immediately from (
2.6
). Now let
|m|\geq 2
. We appeal to the Rayleigh–Ritz quotient: let
f:[0,\pi]\to\mathbb{R}
, then

\Lambda_{m|m|}=\inf_{e^{im\widetilde{\varphi}}f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|\partial_{\theta}f|^{2}\sin\theta+(\frac{1}{4}\sin^{2}\theta+\sin^{-2}\theta)\sin\theta|f|^{2}\,d\theta d\widetilde{\varphi}}{\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|f|^{2}\,\sin\theta\,d\theta d\widetilde{\varphi}}

Let
f(\theta)=\sin\theta
, then we obtain via the above formula,

m^{-2}\Lambda_{m|m|}\leq\frac{17}{10}+\frac{1}{2m^{2}}.

Hence,

m^{-2}\alpha_{m|m|}\leq-\frac{3}{10}+\frac{3}{4m^{2}}.

For
|m|\geq 2
, the above expression therefore implies that

m^{-2}\alpha_{m|m|}\leq-\frac{9}{80}.

∎

We will denote
d\sigma=\sin\theta d\theta d\varphi_{*}
and
d\sigma=\sin\theta d\theta d\widetilde{\varphi}
when integrating functions along constant
(\tau,r)
spheres with respect to
(\tau,r,\theta,\varphi_{*})
and
(\tau,r,\theta,\widetilde{\varphi})
coordinates, respectively.

Let
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
. Then we can write
f=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}f_{m}
, with

f_{m}(\theta,\widetilde{\varphi})=\left[\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}f(\theta,\widetilde{\varphi}^{\prime})e^{-im\widetilde{\varphi}^{\prime}}\,\sin\theta d\theta d\widetilde{\varphi}^{\prime}\right]e^{im\widetilde{\varphi}}.

We refer to
f_{m}
as the
m
-th azimuthal mode of
f
. We can straightforwardly extend the definition of
f_{m}
to functions on
\mathcal{R}
.

Note that the eigenfunctions
S_{m\ell}(\theta)
and the eigenvalues
\Lambda_{+,m\ell}
may be interpreted as the oblate spheroidal harmonics and angular frequencies evaluated at the frequency
\omega=m\upomega_{+}
corresponding to a Carter separation of the Fourier transform of
\phi
in time
t
; see §
6.1
.

2.5.
Notation

We will make use of the notation
O(|f|)
to indicate functions on
\mathcal{M}
that can be bounded uniformly by
|f|
with a constant that depends only on
M
and
\mathbbm{h}
. We denote with
O_{n}(|f|)
functions
g
on
\mathcal{M}
such that
\partial_{r}^{k}g
is bounded uniformly by
|\partial_{r}^{k}f|
for all
k\leq n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

We use the notation
O^{m}_{k}(|f|)
for functions with azimuthal angular dependence
e^{im\widetilde{\varphi}}
, when the uniform constant is allowed to depend additionally on
m
.

We will use the notation
c
and
C
to indicate constants that depend only on
M,a
and
\mathbbm{h}
. If the constants depends on an additional set of parameters
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{s}\in\mathbb{C}
, we will instead use the notation
c_{\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{s}}
and
C_{\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{s}}
. To simplify notation, we make use the following “algebra of constants”:

C\cdot C=C+C=C,\quad c\cdot c=c+c=c.

3.
Preliminaries: wave equation

3.1.
The wave operator

In this section, we express the wave operator
\square_{g}
using different sets of vector fields and we state the relevant local-in-time weighted energy estimates.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\phi
be a solution to the inhomogeneous wave equation:

\square_{g_{M,a}}\phi=G.

(3.1)

Then, we can express (
3.1
) in the following different ways (interpreted in a distributional sense):

(i)

In terms of the vector fields
(T,Y)
, we can express:

\rho^{2}G=Y(\Delta Y\phi)+2aY\Phi\phi+\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\phi+2(r^{2}+a^{2})TY\phi+a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta T^{2}\phi+2aT\Phi\phi+2rT\phi.

(3.2)

(ii)

In terms of the vector fields
(T,X)
, we can express:

\begin{split}\rho^{2}G=&\>X(\Delta X\phi)+2aX\Phi\phi+\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\phi+2(r^{2}+a^{2}-\mathbbm{h}\Delta)TX\phi+[a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta+\mathbbm{h}(\Delta\mathbbm{h}-2(r^{2}+a^{2}))]T^{2}\phi\\
&\>+2a(1-\mathbbm{h})T\Phi\phi+(2r-\frac{d}{dr}(\Delta\mathbbm{h}))T\phi\\
=&\>X(\Delta X\phi)+2aX\Phi\phi+\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\phi-2(r^{2}+a^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}(1+O(r^{-1}))TX\psi\\
&\>+[a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta+\mathbbm{h}(\Delta\mathbbm{h}-2(r^{2}+a^{2}))]T^{2}\phi+2a(1-\mathbbm{h})T\Phi\phi+O(1)T\phi.\end{split}

(3.3)

(iii)

In terms of the vector fields
(K,Y)
, we can express:

\rho^{2}G=Y(\Delta Y\phi)-2\upomega_{+}(r^{2}-r_{+}^{2})Y\Phi\phi+(\not{\mathcal{D}}-2a\upomega_{+}\Phi^{2})\phi-2\upomega_{+}r\Phi\phi+2(r^{2}+a^{2})KY\phi\\
+a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta(K^{2}\phi-2\upomega_{+}K\Phi\phi)+2aK\Phi\phi+2rK\phi.

(3.4)

(iv)

In terms of the vector fields
(K,\widetilde{X})
and the rescaled quantity
\psi:=(r^{2}+a^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}\phi
, we can express:

\rho^{2}G=\widetilde{X}(\Delta\widetilde{X}\phi)-2\upomega_{+}(r^{2}-r_{+}^{2})\widetilde{X}\Phi\phi+(\not{\mathcal{D}}-2a\upomega_{+}\Phi^{2})\phi-2\upomega_{+}r\Phi\phi+2(r^{2}+a^{2}-\mathbbm{h}\Delta)K\widetilde{X}\phi\\
+[a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta+\mathbbm{h}(\Delta\mathbbm{h}-2(r^{2}+a^{2}))]K^{2}\phi+2(a+\mathbbm{h}\upomega_{+}(r^{2}-r_{+}^{2})-a^{2}\upomega_{+}\sin^{2}\theta)K\Phi\phi+\left(2r-\frac{d}{dr}(\Delta\mathbbm{h})\right)K\phi\\
=\widetilde{X}(\Delta\widetilde{X}\phi)-2\upomega_{+}(r^{2}-r_{+}^{2})\widetilde{X}\Phi\phi+(\not{\mathcal{D}}-2a\upomega_{+}\Phi^{2})\phi-2\upomega_{+}r\Phi\phi-2(r+O(r^{-1}))KX\psi\\
+[a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta+O(1)]K^{2}\phi+2(O(1)-a^{2}\upomega_{+}\sin^{2}\theta)K\Phi\phi+O(1)K\phi.

(3.5)

(v)

In terms of the principal null fields
(L,\underline{L})
, we can express:

(r^{2}+a^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}\rho^{2}G=-4\frac{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}}{\Delta}L\underline{L}\psi+a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta K^{2}\psi+2a(1-a\upomega_{+}\sin^{2}\theta)K\Phi\psi+(\not{\mathcal{D}}-2a\upomega_{+}\Phi^{2})\psi\\
-2\frac{ar}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\Phi\psi-\frac{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}}{2r\Delta}\frac{d}{dr}\left(r^{2}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{-3}\Delta^{2}\right)\psi.

(3.6)

Proof.

We write:

\square_{g}\phi=\frac{1}{\sqrt{-\det g_{M,a}}}\sum_{0\leq\alpha,\beta\leq 3}\partial_{\alpha}(\sqrt{-\det g_{M,a}}(g^{-1})^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}\phi)

and evaluate this expression in
(v,r,\theta,\varphi_{*})
, using (
2.2
), in order to obtain (i). The remaining equations follow in a straightforward manner by using the relations between
T,K,Y,Y,\widetilde{X}
and
\Phi
from §
2.3
.
∎

By standard local-in-time energy estimates for linear wave equations, we obtain global existence and uniqueness for solutions to the initial value problem corresponding to (
3.1
); see also
[
AAG23
, Proposition 3.4]
.

Theorem 3.2
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
G\in L^{1}_{\tau}H^{k}(\Sigma_{\tau};\mathbb{C})
and
(\phi_{\rm data},\phi_{\rm data}^{\prime})\in H^{k+1}_{\rm loc}(\Sigma_{0};\mathbb{C})\times H^{k}_{\rm loc}(\Sigma_{0};\mathbb{C})
. Then there exists a solution
\phi
to (
3.1
) (in the distributional sense), such that for all
\tau\geq 0
:

\displaystyle\phi\in

\displaystyle\>C^{0}([0,\tau],H^{k+1}_{\rm loc}(\Sigma_{0};\mathbb{C})\cap C^{1}([0,\tau],H^{k}_{\rm loc}(\Sigma_{0};\mathbb{C}),

\displaystyle\phi|_{\Sigma_{0}}=

\displaystyle\>\phi_{\rm data},

\displaystyle K\phi|_{\Sigma_{0}}=

\displaystyle\>\phi_{\rm data}^{\prime}.

Furthermore, if
(\phi_{\rm data},\phi_{\rm data}^{\prime})\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Sigma_{0};\mathbb{C})\times C^{\infty}_{c}(\Sigma_{0};\mathbb{C})
, then
\phi\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{R};\mathbb{C})
and for all
j,k,l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

\lim_{R\to\infty}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r=R\}}(r^{2}X)^{j}K^{k}\Phi^{l}\psi d\sigma<\infty,

with
\psi
defined in
(v)
of Lemma
3.1
.

In the
a=|M|
case, we define the following
energy densities
, with weights near
r=r_{+}=M
:

\mathcal{E}_{p}[\phi]:=(1-Mr^{-1})^{2-p}|X\phi|^{2}+|T\phi|^{2}+r^{-2}|\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\phi|^{2}\quad\textnormal{with $0\leq p\leq 2$}.

(3.7)

Proposition 3.3
.

Assume
a=|M|
. Let
T_{0}\geq 0
and let
\phi
be a solution to (
3.1
) with
G\in H^{k}(\Sigma_{\tau};\mathbb{C})
for all
\tau\in[0,T_{0}]
, such that
\int_{\Sigma_{0}}\mathcal{E}_{p}[\phi]\,r^{2}d\sigma dr<\infty
for some
0\leq p\leq 2
. Then there exists a constant
C_{T_{0}}>0
such that for all
\tau\in[0,T_{0}]

\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\mathcal{E}_{p}[\phi]\,r^{2}d\sigma dr+\int_{\mathcal{I}^{+}\cap\{0\leq\tau^{\prime}\leq\tau\}}|T\psi_{m}|^{2}\,d\sigma d\tau+\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}\cap\{0\leq\tau^{\prime}\leq\tau\}}|K\psi_{m}|^{2}\,d\sigma d\tau\\
\leq C_{T_{0}}\left[\int_{\Sigma_{0}}\mathcal{E}_{p}[\phi]\,r^{2}d\sigma dr+\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}|G|^{2}r^{2}\,d\sigma drd\tau\right].

Proof.

Let
V_{p}
be a smooth vector field such that
V=L+(r-M)^{-p}\underline{L}
in
r\leq 3M
and
N=T
in
r\geq 4M
. Then

\begin{split}g_{M,\pm|M|}(V_{p},V_{p})=&\>2(r-M)^{-p}g(L,\underline{L})=(r-M)^{-p}g(L+\underline{L},L+\underline{L})\\
=&\>(r-M)^{-p}g\left(T+\frac{M}{r^{2}+M^{2}}\Phi,T+\frac{a}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\Phi\right)\\
=&\>\rho^{-2}(r-M)^{-p}(r^{2}+M^{2})^{-1}\big[M^{2}(r^{2}+M^{2})\sin^{2}\theta-\Delta(r^{2}+M^{2})-4M^{3}r\sin^{2}\theta\\
&\>+M^{2}(r^{2}+M^{2})\sin^{2}\theta-M^{4}(r^{2}+M^{2})^{-1}\Delta\sin^{4}\theta\big]\\
=&\>-\rho^{-2}(r-M)^{2-p}(r^{2}+M^{2}\cos(2\theta)+M^{4}(r^{2}+M^{2})^{-1}\sin^{4}\theta).\end{split}

Hence,
V_{p}
is timelike in
\{r>M\}
for all
0\leq p\leq 2
, timelike along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
if
p=2
and null along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
if
p<2
.

It then follows that:

\mathbb{T}[\phi](V_{p},\mathbf{n}_{\tau})\sim\mathcal{E}_{p}[\phi],

with
\mathbb{T}[\phi]:=\Re(d\phi\otimes d\overline{\phi})-\frac{1}{2}|\nabla\phi|^{2}g
the energy-momentum tensor corresponding to
\square_{g}\phi
and
\mathbf{n}_{\tau}
the unit normal to
\Sigma_{\tau}
.
∎

The main theorems concern solutions to:

\square_{g_{M,a}}\phi=0

(3.8)

with
|a|=M
.

3.2.
Stationary solutions in extremal Kerr

A key role in the analysis will be played by
stationary
,
K
-invariant solutions to to (
3.1
) with
G=0
and
|a|=M
. If we express these with respect to the coordinates
(t,r,\theta,\varphi)
, they take the form

\mathfrak{w}_{m\ell}(r)e^{-i\upomega_{+}t+im\varphi}S_{m\ell}(\theta).

Assume for the sake of convenience that
r_{+}=1
, then

K(\mathfrak{w}_{m\ell}e^{-i\upomega_{+}t+im\varphi}S_{m\ell})=0\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\square_{g_{M,a}}(\mathfrak{w}_{m\ell}e^{-i\upomega_{+}t+im\varphi}S_{m\ell})=0.

is equivalent to the following ODE for
\mathfrak{w}_{m\ell}(r(r_{*}))
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{w}^{\prime\prime}-\widetilde{V}_{\rm stat}(r(r_{*}))\mathfrak{w}=

\displaystyle\>0,

(3.9)

\displaystyle\widetilde{V}_{\rm stat}(r):=

\displaystyle\>-\frac{1}{4}m^{2}+(r^{2}+1)^{-2}\left[m^{2}a^{2}(2r-1)+\Lambda_{+,m\ell}\Delta\right]+(r^{2}+1)^{-3}\Delta(3r^{2}-4r+1)

\displaystyle-3(r^{2}+1)^{-4}\Delta^{2}r^{2}.

Denote
\alpha_{m\ell}=\Lambda_{+,m\ell}-2m^{2}+\frac{1}{4}
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
r_{+}=1
and
a=1
. Assume that there exist
b,B>0
such that
\Lambda_{+,m\ell}\leq Bm^{2}
and
\alpha_{m\ell}\neq 0
for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
. Then for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
, there exists two unique solutions
\mathfrak{w}_{\pm,m\ell}
to (
6.5.8
) that satisfy:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{w}_{\pm,m\ell}(r)=

\displaystyle\>(r-1)^{-\frac{1}{2}\pm i\sqrt{-\alpha_{m\ell}}}\left[1+O_{\infty}((r-1))\right].

(3.10)

Furthermore,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{w}_{\pm,m\ell}^{-1}\frac{d\mathfrak{w}_{\pm,m\ell}}{dr}=

\displaystyle\>-\frac{1}{2}(r-1)^{-1}\pm i\sqrt{-\alpha_{m\ell}}(r-1)^{-1}+O(1).

(3.11)

When
\alpha_{m\ell}=0
, there exist again two unique solutions
\mathfrak{w}_{\pm}
, satisfying:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{w}_{-,m\ell}(r)=

\displaystyle\>(r-1)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left[1+O_{\infty}^{m}((r-1))\right],

(3.12)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{w}_{+,m\ell}(r)=

\displaystyle\>(r-1)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\log\left(\frac{1}{r-1}\right)[1+O_{\infty}^{m}((r-1)]

(3.13)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{w}_{-,m\ell}^{-1}\frac{d\mathfrak{w}_{-,m\ell}}{dr}=

\displaystyle\>-\frac{1}{2}(r-1)^{-1}(r-1)^{-1}+O^{m}(1),

(3.14)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{w}_{+,m\ell}^{-1}\frac{d\mathfrak{w}_{+,m\ell}}{dr}=

\displaystyle\>-\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{\log(r-1)^{-1}}\right)(r-1)^{-1}+\log(r-1)O^{m}(1).

(3.15)

Proof.

We will suppress the subscript
\pm,m\ell
in
\mathfrak{w}_{\pm,m\ell}
below. Note first that we can express (
3.10
) as follows:

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}\mathfrak{w}}{dr^{2}}+2(r-1)^{-1}[1+O_{\infty}((r-1))]\frac{d\mathfrak{w}}{dr}-(r-1)^{-4}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}\widetilde{V}_{\rm stat}\mathfrak{w}=0.

By applying (
6.5.8
) and expanding
\widetilde{V}_{\rm stat}
in
r-1
around
r=1
(see (
6.28
)), it follows that:

\frac{d^{2}\mathfrak{w}}{dr^{2}}+2(r-1)^{-1}[1+O_{\infty}((r-1))]\frac{d\mathfrak{w}}{dr}+(r-1)^{2}\left[2m^{2}-\Lambda_{+,m\ell}+O_{\infty}(r-1)\right]\mathfrak{w}=0.

so
r=1
is a regular singular point for (
6.5.8
) and we can apply the Liouville–Green or WKB approximation in
[
Olv74
, §6.2, Theorem 2.2]
to obtain (
3.10
) and (
3.11
). Here we make use of the condition
\alpha_{m\ell}\neq 0
to ensure that the Liouville–Green approximation does not break down.

In the case
\alpha_{m\ell}\neq 0
, we instead apply standard ODE theory for expansions of solutions around regular singular points
[
Olv74
, §5.4, Theorem 4.1]
which introduces
m
dependence in the expansions (
3.12
)–(
3.15
).
∎

We now introduce the functions
w_{\pm,m\ell}
, which are related to
\mathfrak{w}_{\pm,m\ell}
, defined in Lemma
3.4
, as follows:

w_{\pm,m\ell}(r)e^{-i\upomega_{+}\tau+im\tilde{\varphi}}=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{(r^{2}+1)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\mathfrak{w}_{\pm,m\ell}e^{-i\upomega_{+}t+im\varphi}.

By Lemma
3.4
, we can expand uniformly in
m
for
\alpha_{m\ell}\neq 0
:

\displaystyle w_{\pm}(r)=

\displaystyle\>(r-1)^{-\frac{1}{2}-im\pm i\sqrt{2m^{2}-\Lambda_{+,m\ell}-\frac{1}{4}}}\left[1+O_{\infty}((r-1))\right],

(3.16)

\displaystyle w_{\pm,m\ell}^{-1}\frac{dw_{\pm,m\ell}}{dr}=

\displaystyle\>-\frac{1}{2}(r-1)^{-1}+i\left(-m\pm\sqrt{2m^{2}-\Lambda_{+,m\ell}-\frac{1}{4}}\right)(r-1)^{-1}+O_{\infty}(1).

(3.17)

Furthermore, there exist constants
A_{\pm,m\ell},\widetilde{A}_{\pm,m\ell}\in\mathbb{C}
such that the following expansions hold uniformly in
m
in
r\geq 2
:

w_{\pm,m\ell}=\frac{\widetilde{A}_{\pm,m\ell}}{r}+\frac{{A}_{\pm,m\ell}e^{imr_{*}}}{r}+O_{\infty}(r^{-2}).

(3.18)

When
2m^{2}-\Lambda_{+,m\ell}-\frac{1}{4}=0
, there exist again two unique solutions
w_{\pm,m\ell}
, satisfying (
3.18
) and:

\displaystyle w_{-,m\ell}(r)=

\displaystyle\>(r-1)^{-\frac{1}{2}-im}\left[1+O_{\infty}^{m}((r-1))\right],

(3.19)

\displaystyle w_{+,m\ell}(r)=

\displaystyle\>(r-1)^{-\frac{1}{2}-im}\log\left(\frac{1}{r-1}\right)[1+O_{\infty}^{m}((r-1)],

(3.20)

\displaystyle w_{-,m\ell}^{-1}\frac{dw_{-,m\ell}}{dr}=

\displaystyle\>-\frac{1}{2}(1+2im)(r-1)^{-1}+O_{\infty}^{m}(1),

(3.21)

\displaystyle w_{+,m\ell}^{-1}\frac{dw_{+,m\ell}}{dr}=

\displaystyle\>-\left(\frac{1}{2}+im+(\log(r-1)^{-1})^{-1})\right)(r-1)^{-1}+\log(r-1)O_{\infty}^{m}(1).

(3.22)

Finally, we denote with
w_{\infty}(r)
and
\widetilde{w}_{\infty}(r)
the linear combinations of
w_{\pm,m\ell}
such that
w_{\infty,m\ell}(r)=r^{-1}e^{imr_{*}}+O(r^{-2})
and
\widetilde{w}_{\infty,m\ell}(r)=r^{-1}+O(r^{-2})
. We can express:

\displaystyle w_{\infty,m\ell}(r)=

\displaystyle\>B_{+}w_{+,m\ell}(r)+B_{-}w_{-,m\ell}(r),

\displaystyle\widetilde{w}_{\infty,m\ell}(r)=

\displaystyle\>\widetilde{B}_{+}w_{+,m\ell}(r)+\widetilde{B}_{-}w_{-,m\ell}(r),

\displaystyle w_{\pm,m\ell}(r)=

\displaystyle\>\widetilde{A}_{\pm,m\ell}\widetilde{w}_{\infty,m\ell}(r)+{A}_{\pm,m\ell}w_{\infty,m\ell}(r),

with
\widetilde{B}_{\pm}=\widetilde{A}_{\pm}(1+A_{\pm}B_{\pm})
or
\widetilde{B}_{\mp}=\widetilde{A}_{\pm}^{-1}A_{\pm}B_{\mp}
.

4.
Precise statements of the main theorems

In this section, we will state precisely the main theorems in the present paper. To be able to state a precise version of Theorem
1.2
, we will introduce the function
\Pi
that will give the leading-order late-time asymptotics of
\phi
globally. We will assume without loss of generality that
a>0
. The
a<0
can be included in the analysis by a redefinition
\varphi_{*}\to-\varphi_{*}
. Then we set (without loss of generality)
r_{+}=1
and
a=1
.

Recall the definition of
\alpha_{m\ell}
:
\alpha_{m\ell}=\Lambda_{+,m\ell}-2m^{2}+\frac{1}{4}
. We will consider in this section
m\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{-1,0,1\}
and
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
l\geq|m|
, such that

\alpha_{m\ell}\neq 0.

(4.1)

Then we define the following function, which will describe the global, late-time asymptotic behaviour of
\phi
:

\displaystyle\Pi(\tau,r,\theta,\varphi)=

\displaystyle\>\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|m|\leq\ell\leq l_{m}\\
\alpha_{m\ell}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\Pi_{m\ell}(\tau,r)e^{im\widetilde{\varphi}}S_{m\ell}(\theta),

where
l_{m}
is the largest value of
\ell
such that
\Lambda_{+,m\ell}\leq 2m^{2}
(or equivalently,
\alpha_{m\ell}\leq\frac{1}{4}
) and
\Pi_{m\ell}
is defined as follows: let
{\mathfrak{h}}_{m\ell}\in\mathbb{C}
and let
A_{\pm},\widetilde{A}_{\pm},B_{\pm},\widetilde{B}_{\pm}
be the constants appearing in the expansions of the stationary solutions defined in §
3.2
, then

(1)

If
\alpha_{m\ell}<0
,
\widetilde{B}_{-}\neq 0
and
\widetilde{B}_{+}\neq 0
, we define:

\Pi_{m\ell}(\tau,r):={\mathfrak{h}}_{m\ell}w_{\infty,m\ell}(r)\left[f_{+}^{m\ell}(\tau,r)+f_{-}^{m\ell}(\tau,r)\right]+{\mathfrak{h}}_{m\ell}\widetilde{w}_{\infty,m\ell}(r)\left[E_{+}f_{+,{\rm exp}}^{m\ell}(\tau,r)+E_{-}f_{-,{\rm exp}}^{m\ell}(\tau,r)\right],

(4.2)

with
E_{+}=-\widetilde{B}_{-}^{-1}B_{-}
and
E_{-}=-\widetilde{B}_{+}^{-1}B_{+}
,
{\mathfrak{h}}_{m\ell}\in\mathbb{C}
, and

\displaystyle f_{\pm}^{m\ell}(\tau,r):=\left(\tau+\frac{4}{r-1}+1\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}\mp\sqrt{\alpha_{m\ell}}+im}(\tau+1)^{-\frac{1}{2}\mp\sqrt{\alpha_{m\ell}}-im},

\displaystyle f_{\pm,{\rm exp}}^{m\ell}(\tau,r):=\left(\tau+\frac{4}{r-1}+e^{r-1}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}\mp\sqrt{\alpha_{m\ell}}+im}(\tau+1)^{-\frac{1}{2}\mp\sqrt{\alpha_{m\ell}}-im}.

(2)

If
\alpha_{m\ell}<0
,
\widetilde{B}_{+}=0
, then
\widetilde{A}_{+}=0
, and we define:

\Pi_{m\ell}(\tau,r):={\mathfrak{h}}_{m\ell}w_{\infty,m\ell}(r)f_{+}^{m\ell}(\tau,r)={\mathfrak{h}}_{m\ell}A_{+}^{-1}w_{+,m\ell}(r)f_{+}^{m\ell}(\tau,r).

(4.3)

(3)

If
\alpha_{m\ell}<0
,
\widetilde{B}_{-}=0
, then
\widetilde{A}_{-}=0
and we define:

\Pi_{m\ell}(\tau,r):={\mathfrak{h}}_{m\ell}w_{\infty,m\ell}(r)f_{-}^{m\ell}(\tau,r)={\mathfrak{h}}_{m\ell}A_{-}^{-1}w_{-,m\ell}(r)f_{-}^{m\ell}(\tau,r).

(4.4)

(4)

If
\alpha_{m\ell}>0
, then we define:

\Pi_{m\ell}(\tau,r):={\mathfrak{h}}_{m\ell}w_{\infty,m\ell}(r)f_{+}^{m\ell}(\tau,r).

(4.5)

Denote

F_{\Pi}:=2(r^{2}+1-\mathbbm{h}\Delta)\widetilde{X}\Pi|_{\Sigma_{0}}+2\left(1+\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{h}(r^{2}-1)-\frac{1}{2}\sin^{2}\theta\right)\Phi\Pi|_{\Sigma_{0}}\\
+\left(\mathbbm{h}(\Delta\mathbbm{h}-2(r^{2}+1))-\sin^{2}\theta\right)K\Pi|_{\Sigma_{0}}-\left(2r-\frac{d}{dr}(\Delta\mathbbm{h})\right)\Pi|_{\Sigma_{0}}-\int_{0}^{\infty}\square_{g}\Pi(\tau^{\prime},r,\theta,\widetilde{\varphi})\,d\tau^{\prime}.

We consider the following initial data quantity

F_{\rm hom}[\phi]=-2(r^{2}+1-\mathbbm{h}\Delta)\widetilde{X}\phi|_{\Sigma_{0}}-2\left(1+\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{h}(r^{2}-1)-\frac{1}{2}\sin^{2}\theta\right)\Phi\phi|_{\Sigma_{0}}\\
-\left(\mathbbm{h}(\Delta\mathbbm{h}-2(r^{2}+1))-\sin^{2}\theta\right)K\phi|_{\Sigma_{0}}+\left(2r-\frac{d}{dr}(\Delta\mathbbm{h})\right)\phi|_{\Sigma_{0}}

and the following initial data energy norms along
\Sigma_{0}
.

D^{(1)}[\phi]=\sum_{|m|\leq\ell\leq l_{m}}|{\mathfrak{h}}_{m\ell}|^{2}+\sum_{k+l\leq 4,l\leq 2}\int_{\Sigma_{0}}\mathcal{E}_{1+\epsilon}[K^{k+l}\phi]\,r^{2}d\sigma dr\\
+\int_{\Sigma_{0}\cap\{r\geq 2\}}\sum_{k\leq 2}r^{2-\delta}|LK^{k}\psi|^{2}+\sum_{k\leq 1}r^{2-\delta}|L((r^{2}L)K^{k}\psi)|^{2}\,d\sigma dr\\
+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\leq 2\\
j\leq 1\end{subarray}}\sup_{r^{\prime}\leq 2}\int_{\Sigma_{0}\cap\{r=r^{\prime}\}}|\widetilde{X}^{j}((r-1)\widetilde{X})^{k}F_{\rm hom}[\phi])|^{2}\,d\sigma+\sup_{r^{\prime}\geq 2}\int_{\Sigma_{0}\cap\{r=r^{\prime}\}}|rF_{\rm hom}[\phi]|^{2}\,d\sigma\\
+\int_{\Sigma_{0}\cap\{r\geq 2\}}r^{2}|\widetilde{X}F_{\rm hom}[\phi]|^{2}\,d\sigma dr

and

D_{2}^{(1)}[\phi]=\int_{\Sigma_{0}\cap\{r\geq r_{I}\}}r|L(r^{2}L)^{2}K\widehat{\psi}_{|m|\geq 2}|^{2}\,d\sigma dr+D^{(1)}[\phi]+D^{(1)}[K\phi].

Let
r_{0}>1
and let
\mathfrak{h}_{m\ell}
be chosen such that the following cancellation occurs:

\int_{1}^{\infty}w_{\infty,m\ell}(r)e^{-im\int_{r_{0}}^{r}\frac{x+1}{x-1}\,dx}((F_{\Pi})_{m\ell}+(F_{\rm hom})_{m\ell}[\phi])\,dr\neq 0.

To guarantee this can be done for general initial data, we need that (for
\mathfrak{h}_{m\ell}\neq 0
):

\int_{1}^{\infty}w_{\infty,m\ell}(r)e^{-im\int_{r_{0}}^{r}\frac{x+1}{x-1}\,dx}(F_{\Pi})_{m\ell}\,dr\neq 0.

We will assume, without loss of generality, that the above condition holds for all
\ell\leq l_{m}
. If it did not, we could modify the functions
f_{\pm}^{m\ell}
and
f_{\pm,{\rm exp}}^{m\ell}
by replacing
\left(\tau+\frac{4}{r-1}+1\right)
with
\left(\tau+\frac{4}{r-1}+\beta\right)
and
\left(\tau+\frac{4}{r-1}+e^{r-1}\right)
with
\left(\tau+\frac{4}{r-1}+\beta e^{r-1}\right)
, respectively, and choose
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
appropriately, as this modification does not affect the leading-order asymptotic behaviour of
f_{\pm}^{m\ell}
and
f_{\pm,{\rm exp}}^{m\ell}
.

Observe that
\mathfrak{h}_{m\ell}
is therefore a constant multiple of
\int_{1}^{\infty}w_{\infty,m\ell}(r)e^{-im\int_{r_{0}}^{r}\frac{x+1}{x-1}\,dx}(F_{\rm hom})_{m\ell}[\phi]\,dr
.

Define

\mathfrak{h}_{m}:=\sqrt{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|m|\leq\ell\leq l_{m}\\
\alpha_{m\ell}<0\end{subarray}}|{\mathfrak{h}}_{m\ell}|^{2}}.

By Lemma
2.1
, the set of
\ell
for which
\alpha_{m\ell}<0
is non-empty for all
m
, so
\mathfrak{h}_{m}\neq 0
for generic data (i.e. when restricted to the azimuthal number
m
, this will be the complement of a codimension
l_{m}+1-|m|
set of data).

We first state the following precise version of Theorem
1.2
:

Theorem 4.1

(Late-time tails and stability for azimuthal modes on extremal Kerr; precise version)
.

Let
|m|\geq 2
and consider solutions
\phi_{m}
to (
3.8
) arising from initial data satisfying

\sum_{2k+l\leq 2}D_{2}^{(1)}[Q^{l}K^{k}\phi_{m}]<\infty
. Assume that
\alpha_{m\ell}\neq 0
for all
\ell
and define

\nu_{m}:=\min\left(\{\alpha_{m\ell}\,|\,\alpha_{m\ell}>0\}\cup\{1\}\right).

Assume that for all
1<r_{0}<r_{1}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\leq r_{1}\}}|\phi_{m}|^{2}\,d\sigma drd\tau<

\displaystyle\>\infty,

(4.6)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r_{0}\leq r\leq r_{1}\}}|X\phi_{m}|^{2}+|T\phi_{m}|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\phi_{m}|^{2}\,d\sigma drd\tau<

\displaystyle\>\infty.

(4.7)

Let
0<\eta<\frac{\nu_{m}}{4}
be arbitrarily small. Then there exists a constant
C_{m}>0
, such that for all
\tau\geq 0
:

\displaystyle\left|\phi_{m}-\Pi\right|(\tau,r,\theta,\widetilde{\varphi})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\>C_{m}r^{-1}((\tau+1)(1-r^{-1})+1)^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\tau+1)^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\nu_{m}}{4}+\eta}\sqrt{\sum_{2k+l\leq 2}D_{2}^{(1)}[Q^{l}K^{k}\phi]},

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\mathcal{E}_{2}[\phi-\Pi]\,r^{2}d\sigma dr\leq

\displaystyle\>C_{m}(1+\tau)^{-\nu_{m}+\eta}D^{(1)}[\phi].

Proof.

The theorem is proved by combining Proposition
11.2
and Corollary
11.6
, which are proved in §
11
.
∎

We will now arrive at a precise version of Theorem
1.1
. First, Theorem
4.2
below shows that if the global assumptions (
4.6
) and (
4.7
) hold, the azimuthal instability
(a)
must hold.

Theorem 4.2

(The existence of azimuthal instabilities on extremal Kerr; precise version)
.

Let
|m|\geq 2
and consider solutions
\phi_{m}
satisfying the assumptions from Theorem
4.1
. If there exists an
|m|<\ell_{*}\leq l_{m}
such that
\alpha_{m\ell_{*}}=0
, restrict to initial data for which correspondingly
\mathfrak{h}_{m\ell_{*}}=0
.

(i)

Let
\eta>0
be suitably small. Then there exist constants
c_{m}>0
and
C_{m}>0
, such that

\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\mathcal{E}_{2}[\phi_{m}]\,r^{2}d\sigma dr\geq c_{m}\mathfrak{h}_{m}^{2}-C_{m}(1+\tau)^{-\nu_{m}+\eta}D^{(1)}[\phi_{m}].

(ii)

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
. There exists a constant
c_{m}=c_{m}(m,k)>0
and a monotonically increasing sequence
\{\tau_{n}\}
in
\mathbb{R}_{+}
with
\tau_{n}\to\infty
along which

||Y^{k}\phi_{m}||^{2}_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau_{n}}\cap\mathcal{H}^{+})}\geq\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{n}}\cap\mathcal{H}^{+}}|Y^{k}\phi_{m}|^{2}\,d\sigma\geq c_{m}\mathfrak{h}_{m}^{2}(1+\tau_{n})^{1+2k}.

Proof.

The statements (i) and (ii) follow from a combination of Propositions
12.1
,
12.2
and
12.3
.
∎

From Theorem
4.2
, it follows easily deduce a general instability result, which includes the instability scenarios (b) and (c) of Theorem
4.1
.

Corollary 4.3
.

There exist smooth, compactly supported initial data
(\phi_{*}|_{\Sigma},K\phi_{*}|_{\Sigma})
, such that the corresponding solution
\phi_{*}
satisfies at least one of the following instabilities:

(a)

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
. There exists a constant
b>0
, depending on initial data, and two sequences of times
\{\tau_{n}\}
and
\{\tau_{n}^{\prime}\}
with
\tau_{n},\tau_{n}^{\prime}\to\infty
, such that

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{n}^{\prime}}}\mathcal{E}_{2}[\phi_{*}]\,r^{2}d\sigma dr

\displaystyle\>\geq b^{2},

(4.8)

\displaystyle||Y^{k}\phi_{*}||_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau_{n}}\cap\mathcal{H}^{+})}

\displaystyle\>\geq b\tau_{n}^{\frac{1}{2}+k}.

(4.9)

(b)

There exists an
|m|\geq 2
for which

(\phi_{*})_{m}\notin L^{2}_{\tau}L^{2}(\Sigma_{\tau}).

(4.10)

(c)

There exists an
|m|\geq 2
and radii
1<r_{0}\leq r_{1}<\infty
for which

|(Y\phi_{*})_{m}|+|(K\phi_{*})_{m}|+|(\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\phi_{*})_{m}|\notin L^{2}_{\tau}L^{2}(\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r_{0}\leq r\leq r_{1}\}).

(4.11)

Furthermore, the subset of smooth compactly supported data which does
not
satisfy any of the instabilities (a), (b) and (c) has infinite codimension.

Proof.

Suppose that there do not exist smooth, compactly supported data for which the instabilities (b) and (c) hold. Then for all
|m|\geq 2
, smooth compactly supported data supported on the
m
th azimuthal mode must satisfy (
4.6
) and (
4.7
). We then take
(\phi_{*}|_{\Sigma},K\phi_{*}|_{\Sigma})
to be any smooth and compactly supported data with
\mathfrak{h}_{m|m|}\neq 0
. If
\alpha_{m\ell_{*}}=0
for some
\ell_{*}>|m|
, we invoke the additional restriction
\mathfrak{h}_{m\ell_{*}}=0
. Then the instability (a) follows from Theorem
4.2
, with
b
proportional to
\mathfrak{h}_{m}
.

To conclude infinite codimensionality, we first observe that
|m|\geq 2
above was arbitrary, so we can in fact find an infinite linearly independent set of data, supported on different
m
, with each element resulting in instability (a).

Consider now a solution
\phi
arising from smooth, compactly supported data
(\phi|_{\Sigma},K\phi|_{\Sigma})
, for which none of the instabilities (a)–(c) hold. By linearity of the wave equation, the data
(\phi|_{\Sigma}+\lambda\phi_{*}|_{\Sigma},K\phi|_{\Sigma}+\lambda K\phi_{*}|_{\Sigma})
, with
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, correspond to the solution
\phi+\lambda\phi_{*}
. If
\phi_{*}
satisfies (b) or (c), then so will
\phi+\lambda\phi_{*}
for any
\lambda\neq 0
.

Suppose that
\phi_{*}
satisfies (a) with the sequences
\{\tau_{n}\}
and
\{\tau_{n}^{\prime}\}
. If there exists a
\lambda_{*}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\phi+\lambda_{*}\phi_{*}
does not satisfy (
4.8
) (along any subsequence of
\{\tau_{n}^{\prime}\}
), then there exists a subsequence
\{\tau_{n_{l}}^{\prime}\}
of
\{\tau_{n}^{\prime}\}
along which
\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{n_{l}}^{\prime}}}\mathcal{E}_{2}[\phi+\lambda_{*}\phi_{*}]\,r^{2}d\sigma dr\to 0
as
l\to\infty
. By taking a further subsequence, it follows that
\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\mathcal{E}_{2}[\phi+\lambda\phi_{*}]\,r^{2}d\sigma dr\geq\frac{b^{2}(\lambda-\lambda_{*})^{2}}{2}
along a subsequence of
\{\tau_{n}^{\prime}\}
.

Similarly, we can conclude that if for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\lambda_{*}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\phi+\lambda_{*}\phi_{*}
does not satisfy (
4.9
) (along any subsequence), then there exist a sequence of times along which (
4.9
) does hold for
