\phi+\lambda\phi_{*}
with
b
replaced by
\frac{b}{2}|\lambda-\lambda_{*}|
. Hence, the subset of initial data for which (a), (b) and (c) all do not hold has codimension at least 1. Since we can repeat this argument with
\phi_{*}
supported on different azimuthal modes, this subset must have infinite codimension.
∎

5.
A sketch of the proofs and an overview of the main new ideas

In this section, we give an overview of the main steps in the proofs of Theorems
4.1
and
4.2
. We will highlight the new techniques that are introduced in the present paper and sketch how the novel difficulties listed in §
1.3
are overcome. We will again assume for the sake of convenience that
r_{+}=1
and
a=1
.

5.1.
Part I: Integrated energy estimates in frequency space

The first step towards proving energy decay is to control spacetime integrals of the form

\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}r^{-p}(1-r^{-1})^{q}[|\phi_{m}|^{2}+|\partial\phi_{m}|^{2}]\,r^{2}d\sigma dr,

with
p,q>0
appropriate exponents, in terms of a weighted energy flux along
\Sigma_{0}
. In view of the presence of superradiance and trapped null geodesics that cannot be easily localized in physical space, the strategy for controlling the above integrals involves a Fourier transform in time
t
, which introduces a time frequency
\omega
. Here, we make use of the qualitative assumptions (
4.6
) and (
4.7
), which guarantee sufficient integrability of
\phi
and its derivatives to make sense of the Fourier transform. The main estimates in Part I therefore take place in
Fourier space
.

Carter separation and the frequency triple
(\omega,m,\Lambda)

To analyze the Fourier transform, we make use of the
Carter separation property
for (
3.8
), which implies that if we decompose the Fourier transform of
\phi
in
t
into appropriate angular functions
e^{im\varphi}S_{m\ell}(\theta;a\omega)
, we can separate variables and the wave equation becomes equivalent to an ODE for the coefficients
u_{m\ell}(r_{*};\omega)
in the expansion of the Fourier transform, which takes the form of a time-independent Schrödinger equation:

u^{\prime\prime}+(\omega^{2}-V)u=H,

(5.1)

with
V=V_{\ell m}(r_{*};\omega)
a potential function and the inhomogeneity
H=H_{m\ell}(r;\omega)
determined by the choice of initial data for
\phi
and
(\cdot)^{\prime}=\frac{d}{dr*}(\cdot)
. The angular functions
e^{im\varphi}S_{m\ell}
are eigenfunctions of oblate spheroidal operators with eigenvalues
\lambda_{m\ell}
, with
\lambda_{m\ell}=\ell(\ell+1)
in the special case
a=0
; see §
2.4
. The translated angular frequency
\Lambda_{m\ell}=\lambda_{m\ell}+a^{2}\omega^{2}
forms part of the frequency triple
(\omega,m,\Lambda)
that can be used to characterize the relevant phenomena like superradiance and trapping, which are reflected in the different shapse of the potential
V
, depending on
m,\omega,\Lambda
.

Integrated energy estimates are obtained at the level of
u
by multiplying the equation (
5.1
) with
f(r;\omega)u+g(r;\omega)u^{\prime}
, for appropriate (frequency dependent) choices of
f,g
and then integrating by parts in
r
. Different frequency ranges are dealt with using different multipliers that are adapted to the monotonicity properties of
V
in the frequency regimes under consideration.

Key frequency regimes

The main differences with the frequency-space analysis in the sub-extremal setting from
[
DRSR16
]
appear for
near-superradiant frequencies
:

|\omega-m\upomega_{+}|\leq\epsilon\sqrt{\Lambda}

with
\epsilon\ll 1
. These frequencies can be separated into two sub-regimes:
1)

\Lambda\gg m^{2}
and
2)

\Lambda\lesssim m^{2}
. An important fact about regime
1)
is that it does not feature the difficulties associated to the trapping of null geodesics. The main difference with the
|a|<M
case is the need for
additional degeneracies
in the estimates at the event horizon, related to the higher degeneracy of
V-\omega^{2}
at
\omega=m\upomega_{+}
in the
|a|=M
case. The relevant estimates are proved in §
6.5.4
.

In general, an important new difficulty in the
|a|=M
case is that the phenomenon of trapping and superradiance are coupled in regime
2)
. However, since we are assuming
m
to be bounded or fixed, regime
2)
is a
bounded frequency regime
. Trapping of null geodesics affects unbounded
\Lambda
and it is therefore not an issue in regime
2)
in the present paper. See the comments on the unbounded
m
case in §
1.4
.

Bounded, near-superradiant frequencies

The main new techniques introduced in the frequency-space analysis in the present paper are concerned with the regime
2)
for bounded
m
. The strategy here is to express:

u(r)=W^{-1}\left[u_{\rm inf}(r)\int_{1}^{r}u_{\rm hor}(r^{\prime})H(r^{\prime})\,\frac{r^{\prime 2}+a^{2}}{\Delta}dr^{\prime}+u_{\rm hor}(r)\int_{r}^{\infty}u_{\rm inf}(r^{\prime})H(r^{\prime})\,\frac{r^{\prime 2}+a^{2}}{\Delta}dr^{\prime}\right],

(5.2)

with
u_{\rm hor}
and
u_{\rm inf}
solutions to (
5.1
) with
H=0
, with appropriate boundary conditions at
r=r_{+}
and
r=\infty
, respectively. Estimates for
u
follow from estimates for
u_{\rm hor}
and
u_{\rm inf}
, combined with an estimate for
W^{-1}
. The main difficulties, when compared with the sub-extremal setting, are the following:

(1)

As part of the proof of mode stability,
[
TdC20
]
derived uniform bounds for
W^{-1}
. In contrast with the analogous estimate for sub-extremal Kerr in
[
SR15
]
, these bounds feature constants that blow up as
\omega\to m\upomega_{+}
. Indeed, in
[
TdC20
, Proposition 6.3]
it is shown that

|W^{-1}|\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\omega-m\upomega_{+}|}}.

(2)

In the limit
\omega\to m\upomega_{+}
,
(V-\omega^{2})(r)
has a quadratic zero at
r=r_{+}
, whereas it is linear in the sub-extremal case. For this reason,
r=r_{+}
changes character from an
irregular
singular point to a
regular
singular point of (
5.1
).

Since the singular point
r=r_{+}
or
r_{*}=-\infty
of the ODE (
5.1
) changes character at
m\upomega_{+}
, one can not appeal directly to standard ODE estimates to derive pointwise bounds for
u_{\rm inf}
and
u_{\rm hor}
for
r\in(r_{+},\infty)
. By considering instead the resulting ODE with respect to the rescaled variable
x:=\frac{|\omega-m\upomega_{+}|r_{*}}{|m|}
, it is possible to obtain uniform pointwise estimates in the near-horizon region
|r_{*}|\gtrsim\frac{|m|}{|\omega-m\upomega_{+}|}
that do not breakdown in the limit
\omega\to m\upomega_{+}
(however, the corresponding region in space shrinks in the limit).

We are left with obtaining estimates for the homogeneous solutions
u_{\rm inf}
in the region
|r_{*}|\lesssim\frac{|m|}{|\omega-m\upomega_{+}|}
. Here we face the difficulty that
V-\omega^{2}

changes sign arbitrarily close to
r=r_{+}
for
|\omega-m\upomega_{+}|
sufficiently small. For this reason, it is significantly more difficult to derive integrated estimates in the bounded frequency regime with
|\omega-m\upomega_{+}|\ll 1
, compared to the bounded frequency regime with
|\omega|\ll 1
. Indeed at
\omega=0
, the singular point at
r=\infty
similarly changes character from an irregular to a regular singular point. However, in that case,
V(r)\sim\Lambda r^{-2}
as
r\to\infty
, so one can exploit that
V-\omega^{2}
is positive for large but finite
r
-intervals and
\omega^{2}
suitably small.

The key idea for resolving the difficulty of the change in sign of
V-\omega^{2}
is to rescale
u
by considering

\check{u}_{\pm,\rm inf}=\frac{u_{\rm inf}}{\mathfrak{w}_{\pm}},

where
\mathfrak{w}_{\pm}
give the radial part of the
K
-independent solutions to (
3.8
) that were introduced in §
3.2
. In frequency space,
\mathfrak{w}_{\pm}
can be considered solutions to (
5.1
) with
\omega=m\upomega_{+}
, with
H\equiv 0
and appropriate boundary conditions at
r=r_{+}
. The effect of this rescaling is that the ODE satisfied by
\check{u}_{\pm,\rm inf}
features a potential which factors of
\omega-m\upomega_{+}
that has a good sign in the region
|r_{*}|\lesssim\frac{|m|}{|\omega-m\upomega_{+}|}
. A physical space analogue of this rescaling trick was used in the integrated energy estimates of
[
Gaj22
]
. The analysis of
\check{u}_{\pm,\rm hor}
and
\check{u}_{\pm,\rm inf}
is carried out in §
6.5.8
.

Employing the identity (
5.2
), together with estimates for
u_{\rm inf}
in the region
|r_{*}|\gtrsim\frac{|m|}{|\omega-m\upomega_{+}|}
and global estimates for
u_{\rm inf}
, obtained via the strategy above, then results in estimates for
u(r)
that are also valid in the region of the form
|r_{*}|\gtrsim\frac{|m|}{|\omega-m\upomega_{+}|}
. Extending these to
|r_{*}|\lesssim\frac{|m|}{|\omega-m\upomega_{+}|}
involves considering again the rescaled variables
\check{u}_{\pm}=\frac{u}{\mathfrak{w}_{\pm}}
, as discussed in the estimates for
\check{u}_{\pm,\rm inf}
.

The main conclusion of the above estimates in frequency space, when combined with Plancherel’s theorem, is the following integrated energy estimate and energy boundedness estimate: for all
\epsilon>0
, there exists a constant
C_{m}>0
, such that

\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{2}}}\mathcal{E}_{1-\epsilon}[\phi]\,r^{2}d\sigma dr+\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\leq 2M\}}(1-Mr^{-1})^{\epsilon}\mathcal{E}_{0}[\phi]+(1-Mr^{-1})^{-1+\epsilon}|K\phi|^{2}\,d\sigma drd\tau\\
\leq C_{m}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{1}}}\mathcal{E}_{1+\epsilon}[\phi]\,r^{2}d\sigma dr,

(5.3)

which is derived in §
7
.

Note in particular that the energy controlled in the spacetime integral on the LHS degenerates at
r=M
and the energy flux term on the LHS features an energy with an additional factor
(1-Mr^{-1})^{2\epsilon}
, when compared to the energy flux term on the RHS. There is no loss of derivatives on the RHS due to the presence of trapped null geodesics, as for bounded
m
, there is no trapping in the region
r\leq 2M
. We complement the above estimate with an integrated energy estimate that does feature the region containing trapped null geodesics and hence loses a derivative on the RHS: let
r_{I}>0
be arbitrarily large, then

\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{2M\leq r\leq r_{I}\}}\mathcal{E}_{2}[\phi]\,d\sigma drd\tau\leq C\sum_{k\leq 1}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{1}}}\mathcal{E}_{1+\epsilon}[K^{k}\phi]\,r^{2}d\sigma dr.

(5.4)

5.2.
Part II: Energy decay,
K
-derivatives and
K
-integrals

The remaining part of the proof concerns energy estimates in physical space, starting from the integrated and energy boundedness estimates (
5.3
) and (
5.4
).

More
K
-derivatives lead to more decay

The first new difficulty one faces when trying to convert the integrated energy estimates (
5.3
) and (
5.4
) into inverse-polynomial decay estimates for (weighted) energies along
\Sigma_{\tau}
is the fact that there is no hierarchy of estimates with weights
(r-M)^{-p}
near
r=M
, as is the case in the region
r\gg M
for energies with
r^{p}
-weights, or in the extremal Reissner–Nordström setting near
r=M
.

Indeed, by applying the mean-value theorem to (
5.3
) and (
5.4
), combined with an
r^{p}
-estimate in
r\gg M
, the best that one can get is
\tau^{-1}
decay for the strongly degenerate energy with energy density
(1-Mr^{-1})^{\epsilon}\mathcal{E}_{0}[\phi]
along some sequence
\{\tau_{n}\}
of times. To get the desired pointwise estimates, one would need decay for all times
\tau\geq 0
, also for less degenerate energies, with better decay rates.

The key idea here is that there is an
alternative way
to obtain a hierarchy of additional, less degenerate estimates near
r=M
, namely, by replacing
\phi
with the time derivative
K\phi
. Indeed, from (
3.8
) if follows that, schematically,

\mathcal{E}_{p}[K\phi]\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{p-2}[(r-M)Y\phi]+\mathcal{E}_{p-2}[\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\phi]+\mathcal{E}_{p-2}[K\phi].

Hence,
K
derivatives can be traded for additional degenerate factors of
r-M
, at the expense of requiring estimates additionally estimates for the derivatives
(r-M)Y\phi
and
\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\phi
. Fortunately, the estimates for
\phi
extend naturally to
(r-M)Y\phi
and
\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\phi
. In
[
Gaj22
]
, energy decay with the desired rates is similarly obtained by replacing
\phi
with time derivatives
T^{k}\phi
. The additional difficulty in the present setting is that, in contrast with
[
Gaj22
]
, there is no (degenerate) energy boundedness estimate available without additional degeneracies on the LHS.

Note that the decay rate obtained by repeatedly applying
K
is limited by the length of the hierarchy of
r^{p}
-estimates near infinity, so it is not possible to obtain arbitrarily fast decay for a suitable number of
K
-derivatives. The energy decay estimates for
K
-derivatives of
\phi
are derived in §
9
.

The difference function
\phi-\Pi

It remains to apply the energy decay estimates for
K\phi
to obtain energy decay estimates for
\phi
. In view of the analogous steps in
[
Gaj22
]
, it would be natural to think of
\phi
as a
K
-derivative:
\phi=K(K^{-1}\phi)
, where, formally,

K^{-1}\phi(\tau,r,\theta,\widetilde{\varphi})=-\int_{\tau}^{\infty}\phi(\tau^{\prime},r,\theta,\widetilde{\varphi})\,d\tau^{\prime}

and use the energy decay estimates for
K
-derivatives from the previous step.

This strategy does not quite work. Indeed, in view of the decay rates in Theorem
1.2
, we will prove
\tau^{-1}
-decay for
\phi
away from
r=M
, but this is not integrable, so the time integral does not make sense.

Instead, we consider

\widehat{\phi}=\phi-\Pi

with
\Pi
the function defined in §
4
, which will encode the precise, global leading-order late-time behaviour of
\phi
. As can be seen in Theorem
4.1
,
\widehat{\phi}

does
decay with an integrable rate in time and we are able to make sense of
K^{-1}\widehat{\phi}
. Since
\widehat{\phi}
satisfies an inhomogeneous wave equation (
3.1
) with
G=-\square_{g}\Pi
, the inhomogeneity has to decay suitably fast in
\tau
and
r
in order to be able to repeat the integrated energy estimates and energy decay estimates for
\widehat{\phi}
instead of
\phi
.

The consideration of
\widehat{\phi}
moreover allows us to go beyond (sharp) upper bound decay estimates for
\phi
and to derive the
precise
leading-order late-time behaviour of
\phi
. Indeed, if we can show that
\widehat{\phi}
decays
faster
than
\Pi
, it means that the leading-order late-time asymptotics or
\phi
coincide with the leading-order late-time asymptotics of
\Pi
.

K
-inversion and elliptic estimates

Since we do not know
a priori
that
\widehat{\phi}
decays suitably fast to be able to make sense of
K^{-1}\phi
, defined as an integral in time, we instead construct the initial data for
K^{-1}\phi
by solving an elliptic type equation:

\mathcal{L}K^{-1}\widehat{\phi}|_{\Sigma_{0}}=F[\phi|_{\Sigma_{0}}],

where
F[\phi|_{\Sigma_{0}}]
can be split as follows:

F[\phi|_{\Sigma_{0}}]=F_{\rm hom}[\phi]+F_{\Pi},

with
F_{\rm hom}[\phi|_{\Sigma_{0}}]
depending on the initial data for
\phi
along
\Sigma_{0}
and
F_{\Pi}
depending only on
\Pi
.

More concretely, we construct the projections
(K^{-1}\widehat{\phi})_{\ell m}
to the angular functions
S_{m\ell}(\theta)
, defined in §
2.4
, and derive uniform elliptic estimates for the operator
\mathcal{L}
for large values of
\ell
to be able to sum
(K^{-1}\widehat{\phi})_{\ell m}
over
\ell
. These elliptic estimates are adapted to the vector fields
(K,\widetilde{X})
and require more decay of the initial data for
\phi
as
r\to\infty
than elliptic estimates adapted to the
(T,X)
vector fields.

The construction of
(K^{-1}\widehat{\phi})_{\ell m}
amounts to solving an ODE. This ODE can easily be solved by considering instead the rescaled variables

\frac{(K^{-1}\widehat{\phi})_{\ell m}}{w_{\infty,m\ell}},

with
w_{\infty,m\ell}(r)
the radial parts of the
K
-invariant stationary solutions introduced in to (
3.8
) that were introduced in §
3.2
, which are closely related to the functions
\mathfrak{w}_{\pm}(r)
described in Part I above; see §
3.2
. The ODE for these rescaled variables can be put in the folllowing form for
a=M=1
:

\widetilde{X}\left(\Delta w_{\infty,m\ell}^{2}e^{-im\int_{r_{0}}^{r}\frac{x+1}{x-1}\,dx}\widetilde{X}\left(\frac{(K^{-1}\widehat{\phi})_{\ell m}}{w_{\infty,m\ell}}\right)\right)=e^{-im\int_{r_{0}}^{r}\frac{x+1}{x-1}\,dx}w_{\infty,m\ell}F_{m\ell},

and can therefore be solved by simply integrating twice in the
\widetilde{X}
-direction. The requirement that the integral of the RHS over
r\in(M,\infty)
vanishes for
\alpha_{m\ell}\leq\frac{1}{4}
, fixes the constants
\mathfrak{h}_{m\ell}
appearing in the definition of
\Pi
and therefore also the initial-data-dependent factors in the coefficients in front of the late-time tails of
\phi
. The decay rates in the late-time tails for
\phi
are then encoded in the behaviour of
(K^{-1}\widehat{\phi})
as
r\downarrow M
. The data corresponding to the
K
-integral of
\phi_{m}
and the corresponding elliptic estimates are derived in §
10
.

5.3.
Part III: Instability follows from stability

Part I and Part II above lead to a proof of Theorem
4.1
, which is the main ingredient in the proof of Theorem
4.2
.

First, non-decay of the non-degenerate energy
\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\mathcal{E}_{2}[\phi]\,r^{2}d\sigma dr
follows from decay of the energy of the difference function
\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\mathcal{E}_{2}[\widehat{\phi}]\,r^{2}d\sigma dr
, combined with
non-decay of

\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\mathcal{E}_{2}[\Pi]\,r^{2}d\sigma dr,

which in turn follows by plugging in the explicit expressions for
\Pi
.

To obtain pointwise blow-up of
Y\phi_{m}
we use that
Y\phi_{m}
satisfies the following equation along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, obtained by simply evaluating (
3.8
) along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
:

K(-4Y\phi_{m\ell})=(\Lambda_{m\ell}+m^{2}-im)\phi_{m\ell}+(\sin^{2}\theta(K^{2}\phi-imK\phi))_{m\ell}+2(1+im)K\phi_{m\ell}.

Since Theorem
4.1
gives the precise late-time tails of
\phi_{m\ell}
and implies that
K^{2}\phi
and
K\phi
decay faster, we integrate the above equation to obtain the precise late-time behaviour of
Y\phi_{m}
. Due to the possible oscillation present in the late-time behaviour of
|Y\phi_{m}|
, it will not satisfy a uniform lower bound, but we can identify a sequence of times
\{\tau_{n}\}
along which blow-up occurs. Stronger blow-up of higher-order
Y
-derivatives can similarly be obtained (along a sequence of times) by plugging in the late-time asymptotics of lower-order
Y
-derivatives into the equation along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
that are obtained by commuting (
3.8
) with
Y^{k}
and evaluating the resulting equation along
\mathcal{H}^{+}
.

The instability results in the paper are all derived in §
12
.

6.
Frequency-space analysis

In this section, we derive the estimates in frequency space that are required to obtain integrated energy estimates. In order to be able to take a Fourier transform, we will introduce a
global assumption
on solutions
\phi
arising from smooth and compactly supported initial data on
\Sigma_{0}
. Without loss of generality, we will assume in this section that
r_{+}=1
and
a\geq 0
. Indeed, the
a<0
can be included in the analysis by transforming
\varphi_{*}\to-\varphi_{*}
.

First we make the following integrability assumption on the inhomogeneity
G
:

Assumption 1
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and let
G_{m}
denote the projection of
G
to the
m
-th azimuthal mode. Then for all
n_{1},n_{2},n_{3}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}|\rho^{2}G_{m}|^{2}\,d\sigma drd\tau<

\displaystyle\>\infty,

(6.1)

\displaystyle\sup_{r\in[r_{0},r_{1}]}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{n_{1}}X^{n_{2}}T^{n_{3}}G_{m}|^{2}(\tau,r,\theta,\varphi)\,d\sigma d\tau<

\displaystyle\>\infty

(6.2)

We make the following global assumption on solutions
\phi
to (
3.1
):

Assumption 2
.

Let
\phi
be a solution to (
3.1
) with
G=0
, arising from initial data
(\phi_{0},\phi_{0}^{\prime})\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Sigma_{0})\times C_{c}^{\infty}(\Sigma_{0})
. Then for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and
r_{1}>r_{0}>r_{+}
:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\leq r_{1}\}}|\phi_{m}|^{2}\,d\sigma drd\tau<

\displaystyle\>\infty,

(6.3)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r_{0}\leq r\leq r_{1}\}}|X\phi_{m}|^{2}+|T\phi_{m}|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\phi_{m}|^{2}\,d\sigma drd\tau<

\displaystyle\>\infty.

(6.4)

Note that for
m=0
and
|a|=M
, the Assumptions
6.3
and
6.4
hold by the decay results in
[
Are12
]
. For
|a|<M
, the Assumptions
6.3
and
6.4
hold for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}
by the decay results in
[
DRSR16
]
.

6.1.
Carter separation

We first introduce the class of
sufficiently integrable functions
:

Definition 6.1
.

A function
f:\mathring{\mathcal{M}}_{M,a}\to\mathbb{C}
is
sufficiently integrable
if: for all
n_{1},n_{2},n_{3}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
r_{1}>r_{0}>r_{+}
:

\displaystyle\sup_{r\in[r_{0},r_{1}]}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\left[|\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{n_{1}}{\mathfrak{X}}_{*}^{n_{2}}T^{n_{3}}f|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{n_{1}}{\mathfrak{X}}_{*}^{n_{2}}T^{n_{3}}\square_{g_{M,a}}f|^{2}\right](t,r_{*},\theta,\varphi)\,d\sigma dt<

\displaystyle\>\infty,

(6.5)

\displaystyle\int_{r_{+}}^{r_{0}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|({\mathfrak{X}}_{*}-K)f|^{2}\,\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta}d\sigma dtdr+\int_{r_{1}}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|({\mathfrak{X}}_{*}+T)f|^{2}\,d\sigma dtdr<

\displaystyle\>\infty.

(6.6)

Now, let
\phi:\mathcal{M}_{M,a}\to\mathbb{C}
be a solution to (
3.1
) with
G=0
arising from smooth and compactly supported initial data, and let
\Pi:\mathcal{M}_{M,a}\to\mathbb{C}
be a function that will be fixed later in §
10.5
. We denote
\widehat{\phi}=\phi-\Pi
and
G=-\square_{g}\Pi
. Note that
\widehat{\phi}
is a solution to (
3.1
) with this choice of
G
.

Let
\xi:\mathcal{M}_{M,a}\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth cut-off function, such that
\xi\equiv 0
in
\{\tau\leq 0\}
and
\xi\equiv 1
in
\{\tau\geq 1\}
. Define:

\upphi=\xi\cdot\widehat{\phi}.

Then

\square_{g_{M,a}}\upphi=F_{\xi}+\xi G=:F,

(6.7)

with

\rho^{2}F_{\xi}=2(r^{2}+a^{2}-\mathbbm{h}\Delta)T(\xi)X\phi+[a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta+\mathbbm{h}(\Delta\mathbbm{h}-2(r^{2}+a^{2}))](2T(\xi)T\phi+T^{2}(\xi)\phi)+2a(1-\mathbbm{h})T(\xi)\Phi\phi\\
+(2r-(\Delta\mathbbm{h})^{\prime})T(\xi)\phi\\
=-2T(\xi)(r^{2}+a^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}(1+O(r^{-1}))X\psi+O(1)T(\xi)\phi+[a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta+\mathbbm{h}(\Delta\mathbbm{h}-2(r^{2}+a^{2}))](2T(\xi)T\phi+T^{2}(\xi)\phi)\\
+2a(1-\mathbbm{h})T(\xi)\Phi\phi\ ,

(6.8)

which is supported in
\{0\leq\tau\leq 1\}
.

Proposition 6.1
.

Assuming (
6.3
) and (
6.4
), the function
\upphi_{m}
satisfying (
6.7
) is sufficiently integrable if
\xi\cdot\Pi
is sufficiently integrable and
G=-\square_{g}\Pi
satisfies (
6.1
).

Proof.

By standard elliptic estimates in the bounded region
1<r_{0}<r_{1}\leq\infty
(away from the event horizon and for bounded
m
) and the fundamental theorem of calculus, we apply the assumption on
\Pi
and (
6.1
) to conclude that

\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}+n_{3}\leq N}\sup_{r\in[r_{0},r_{1}]}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{n_{1}}\partial_{r_{*}}^{n_{2}}\partial_{t}^{n_{3}}\upphi_{m}|^{2}(t,r_{*},\theta,\varphi)\,dt<\infty

holds if (
6.4
) holds with
\phi_{m}
replaced by
K^{n}\phi_{m}
, where
n\leq N
. Since
K^{n}\phi_{m}
is also a solution to (
3.1
) with
G=0
arising from smooth, compactly supported data, (
6.4
) applies also to
K^{n}\phi_{m}
and, applying (
6.1
) once more to estimate
\square_{g}\upphi_{m}
, we conclude that (
6.5
) holds for
f=\upphi_{m}
.

We have additionally that

\int_{r_{1}}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|({\mathfrak{X}}_{*}+T)\upphi|^{2}\,d\sigma dtdr<\infty

by the sufficient integrability assumption on
\Pi
together with a standard
r^{p}
-estimate with
p=1
in the region
r\geq r_{I}
, for
r_{I}
sufficiently large.

We similarly obtain

\int_{r_{1}}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|({\mathfrak{X}}_{*}-K)\upphi|^{2}\,\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta}d\sigma dtdr<\infty

by applying Corollary
8.2
together with Assumption
6.3
.

∎

6.2.
The frequency-localized ODE

For sufficiently integrable
\upphi
, the Fourier transform in
t
along constant
(r,\theta,\varphi)
of
\upphi
is well-defined, and we will denote it by
\widehat{\upphi}
. Then we can express:

\upphi(t,r,\theta,\varphi)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i\omega t}\widehat{\upphi}(\omega,r,\theta,\varphi)\,d\omega,

where the above identity is to be interpreted in the function space
L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}
.

We will moreover define:

\widetilde{\omega}:=\omega-m\upomega_{+}.

The frequency
\omega
is the time-frequency with respect to the flow along
T
, whereas the shifted frequency
\widetilde{\omega}
may be interpreted as the time-frequency with respect to the flow along
K
.

Subsequently, we can decompose
\widehat{\upphi}
as follows in terms of the orthonormal basis of oblate spheroidal harmonics in
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
(see §
2.4
):

\widehat{\upphi}(\omega,r,\theta,\varphi)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\\
|m|\leq\ell\end{subarray}}\widehat{\upphi}^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}(r)S_{m\ell}(\theta;a\omega)e^{im\varphi},

which is to be interpreted as an identity in the function space
L^{2}_{\omega}L^{2}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}
. To simplify the notation, we introduce the shorthand notation:

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\\
|m|\leq\ell\end{subarray}}\to\sum_{m\ell}.

Hence, we obtain the following identity in the space
L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}
:

\upphi(t,r,\theta,\varphi)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sum_{m\ell}\widehat{\upphi}^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}(r)S_{m\ell}(\theta;a\omega)e^{i(m\varphi+\omega t)}\,d\omega.

A remarkable property of the Kerr spacetime is that the equation (
6.7
) reduces to an ODE for the variable
\widehat{\upphi}^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}(r)
. This is known as
Carter’s separation
.

Proposition 6.2

(Carter’s separation)
.

Let
\upphi
be a sufficiently integrable solution to (
6.7
). Then
\widehat{\upphi}^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}
satisfies the following equality in
L^{2}_{\omega}l^{2}_{ml}
:

\Delta(\rho^{2}F)^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}=\Delta\frac{d}{dr}\left(\Delta\frac{d\widehat{\upphi}^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}}{dr}\right)+[a^{2}m^{2}+(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}\omega^{2}-4Mra\omega m-\Delta(\lambda^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}+a^{2}\omega^{2})]\widehat{\upphi}^{(a\omega)}.

Furthermore, if we rescale

\displaystyle u^{a\omega}_{m\ell}:=

\displaystyle\>\sqrt{r^{2}+a^{2}}\widehat{\upphi}^{a\omega}_{m\ell},

\displaystyle H^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}:=

\displaystyle\>\Delta(r^{2}+a^{2})^{-\frac{3}{2}}(\rho^{2}F)^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell},

then
u^{a\omega}_{m\ell}
satisfies the following in
L^{2}_{\omega}l^{2}_{ml}
:

\frac{d^{2}u^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}}{dr_{*}^{2}}+(\omega^{2}-V_{ml}^{(a\omega)})u^{a\omega}_{m\ell}=H^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell},

(6.9)

with

V_{m\ell}^{(a\omega)}=(r^{2}+a^{2})^{-2}\left[4Ma\omega r-a^{2}m^{2}+\Delta(\lambda^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}+a^{2}\omega^{2})\right]+(r^{2}+a^{2})^{-3}\Delta\left[3r^{2}-4Mr+a^{2}\right]\\
-3r^{2}\Delta^{2}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{-4}.

We denote:

\Lambda_{m\ell}^{(a\omega)}=\lambda^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}+a^{2}\omega^{2}.

From §
2.4
, it follows that for
\omega=m\upomega_{+}
:

\Lambda_{m\ell}^{(am\upomega_{+})}=\Lambda_{+,m\ell}.

We moreover have that

\displaystyle\lim_{m\widetilde{\omega}\to 0}\frac{1}{a^{2}m\widetilde{\omega}}(\Lambda_{m\ell}^{(a\omega)}-\Lambda_{m\ell}^{(am\upomega_{+})})<

\displaystyle\>\infty,

(6.10)

\displaystyle\lim_{a\to M}\frac{1}{(a^{2}-M^{2})m^{2}\omega^{2}}\left(\Lambda_{m\ell}^{(a\omega)}-\Lambda_{m\ell}^{(\omega)}\right)<

\displaystyle\>\infty,

(6.11)

by the smooth dependence of
\Lambda_{m\ell}^{(a\omega)}
on
a\omega
; see for example
[
MS54
, §3.22, Proposition 1]
.

6.3.
Boundary conditions

In this section, we will make use of the regularity assumptions at
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and
\mathcal{I}^{+}
encoded in the definition of sufficient integrability in Definition
6.1
, in order to derive boundary conditions for the ODE (
6.9
).

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
\upphi
be a sufficiently integrable solution to (
6.7
). Then
u^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}
satisfies the following boundary conditions: there exists sequences
\{r_{i}\}
and
\{\tilde{r}_{i}\}
, with
r_{i}\to\infty
and
\tilde{r_{i}}\downarrow 1
as
i\to\infty
, such that:

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}\left|\frac{du^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}}{dr_{*}}-i\omega u^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}\right|(r_{i})=

\displaystyle\>0,

(6.12)

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}\left|\frac{du^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}}{dr_{*}}+i\widetilde{\omega}u^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}\right|(\tilde{r}_{i})=

\displaystyle\>0,

(6.13)

for almost every
\omega\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

By (
6.6
) combined with Plancherel, it follows that: for
r_{0}>r_{+}
and
r_{1}<\infty

\int_{r_{+}}^{r_{0}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|\frac{du^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}}{dr_{*}}+i\widetilde{\omega}u^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}\right|^{2}\,d\omega dr+\int_{r_{1}}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|\frac{du^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}}{dr_{*}}-i\omega u^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}\right|^{2}\,d\omega dr<\infty.

Let
\rho_{n}
,
i\geq 1
be a strict monotonically increasing sequence with
\rho_{1}=r_{1}
and
\rho_{i}\to\infty
as
i\to\infty
. By applying the mean-value theorem with
r\in[\rho_{i},\rho_{i+1}]
, we can find a sequence
\rho^{\prime}_{i}
such that
\rho_{i}^{\prime}\to\infty
and

\lim_{i\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|\frac{du^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}}{dr_{*}}-i\omega u^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}\right|^{2}(\rho_{i}^{\prime})\,d\omega=0.

Convergence of a sequence with respect to the
L^{2}_{\omega}(\mathbb{R})
-norm implies that there exists a subsequence along which the sequence convergence pointwise for almost every
\omega
. This concludes (
6.12
).

The limit (
6.13
) follows similarly, by taking instead
\rho_{n}
,
i\geq 1
to be a strict monotonically decreasing sequence with
\rho_{1}=r_{0}
and
\rho_{i}\to 1
as
i\to\infty
.
∎

As the goal of Carter’s separation is to arrive at integrated energy estimates, it is sufficient to restrict the consideration to a subset of frequencies
\omega
with full Lebesgue measure. In the proposition below, we show that the limits in Lemma
6.3
can be interpreted as boundary conditions for smooth solutions to the (
6.9
), for
almost every

\omega\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
\upphi
be a sufficiently integrable solution to (
6.7
). Then, for all
m,\ell
, with
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq-\ell}\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\leq\ell}
, and for almost every
\omega\in\mathbb{R}
:

(i)

H^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}
is smooth on
(\infty,\infty)_{r_{*}}
and the corresponding solution
u^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}
to (
6.9
) is smooth and satisfies the following boundary conditions:

\displaystyle\lim_{r_{*}\to\infty}\left|\frac{du^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}}{dr_{*}}-i\omega u^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}\right|(r_{*})=

\displaystyle\>0,

(6.14)

\displaystyle\lim_{r_{*}\to-\infty}\left|\frac{du^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}}{dr_{*}}+i\widetilde{\omega}u^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}\right|(r_{*})=

\displaystyle\>0.

(6.15)

(ii)

H^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}
satisfies:

\int_{r_{+}}^{\infty}\Delta^{-2}r^{4}|H^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}|^{2}\,dr<\infty.

(6.16)

Proof.

We refer to
[
DRSR16
, Lemma 5.4.1]
for a proof of (i), with the minor modification that we can restrict to the region
r\in[r_{0},\infty)
, since we are proving smoothness in
(-\infty,\infty)_{r_{*}}
.

Note that by Plancherel

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{m\ell}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{r_{+}+\frac{1}{n}}^{n}\Delta^{-2}r^{4}|H^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}|^{2}\,drd\omega=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}r^{-2}|\rho^{2}F|^{2}\,d\sigma drd\tau.

By the local-in-time weighted energy estimates from Proposition
3.3
and the assumption (
6.1
), we can further estimate

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}r^{-2}|\rho^{2}F|^{2}\,d\sigma drd\tau\leq&\>\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}r^{-2}|\rho^{2}F_{\xi}|^{2}\,d\sigma drd\tau+C\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}|rG|^{2}\,d\sigma drd\tau\\
\leq&\>C\int_{\Sigma_{0}}\mathcal{E}_{2}[\widehat{\phi}]\,r^{2}d\sigma dr+C\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}|rG|^{2}\,d\sigma drd\tau<\infty.\end{split}

Hence,
\sum_{m\ell}\int_{r_{+}+\frac{1}{n}}^{n}\Delta^{-2}r^{4}|H^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}|^{2}\,dr
is Cauchy in
L^{1}_{\omega}(\mathbb{R})
and therefore has a subsequence that converges pointwise for almost every
\omega
. As the original sequence is monotonically increasing in
n
, it must also converge pointwise for almost every
\omega
, which concludes (
6.16
).
∎

6.4.
The potential
V_{ml}^{(a\omega)}

In this section, we derive the main properties and decompositions of the potential
V_{ml}^{(a\omega)}
that play a role in the rest of the analysis in this section.

6.4.1.
The main potentials

With the choice
r_{+}=1
, we can express:

\displaystyle M=

\displaystyle\>\frac{1}{2}(1+a),

\displaystyle\upomega_{+}=

\displaystyle\>\frac{a}{1+a^{2}},

\displaystyle\Delta=

\displaystyle\>(r-1)(r-a^{2}).

and
0\leq a\leq 1
remains our only free parameter. In the sections below we will suppress the super- and subscripts in the notation
V_{m\ell}^{(a\omega)}
and
\lambda_{m\ell}^{(a\omega)}
; we will simply write
V
and
\Lambda
, respectively.

The potentials
V_{0}
and
V_{1}

The potential
V(r)
can be split as follows:

\displaystyle V(r)=

\displaystyle\>V_{0}(r)+V_{1}(r),\quad\textnormal{with}

(6.17)

\displaystyle V_{0}(r)=

\displaystyle\>(r^{2}+a^{2})^{-2}\left[2ma(1+a^{2})\omega r-a^{2}m^{2}+\Lambda\Delta\right],

(6.18)

\displaystyle V_{1}(r)=

\displaystyle\>(r^{2}+a^{2})^{-3}\Delta(3r^{2}-2(1+a^{2})r+a^{2})-3(r^{2}+a^{2})^{-4}\Delta^{2}r^{2},

(6.19)

\displaystyle\Lambda:=

\displaystyle\>\lambda+a^{2}\omega^{2}.

Note that
V_{1}\geq 0
.

The potential
\widetilde{V}

We define the shifted potential:

\widetilde{V}-\widetilde{\omega}^{2}:=V-\omega^{2}.

We can express:

\displaystyle\widetilde{V}(r)=

\displaystyle\>\widetilde{V}_{0}(r)+\widetilde{V}_{1}(r),\quad\textnormal{with}

\displaystyle\widetilde{V}_{0}(r)=

\displaystyle\>-2(1+a^{2})^{-1}ma\widetilde{\omega}-a^{2}m^{2}(1+a^{2})^{-2}+(r^{2}+a^{2})^{-2}\left[2ma(1+a^{2})\widetilde{\omega}r+m^{2}a^{2}(2r-1)+\Lambda\Delta\right],

\displaystyle\widetilde{V}_{1}(r)=

\displaystyle\>V_{1}(r).

The potential
V_{q,\varpi}

Let
q,\varpi\in\mathbb{R}
. Consider the following modified potential that arises when considering the ODE for the rescaled variable
v^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}=e^{i(\varpi+\widetilde{\omega})r_{*}}\Delta^{iq}u^{(a\omega)}_{m\ell}
(see §
6.5.1
for the motivation for considering such a rescaling):

V_{q,\varpi}(r):=\widetilde{V}-\widetilde{\omega}^{2}+\left[(\widetilde{\omega}+\varpi)^{2}+2q(\widetilde{\omega}+\varpi)\frac{d\Delta}{dr}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{-1}+q^{2}\left(\frac{d\Delta}{dr}\right)^{2}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{-2}\right]

In order for the leading-order terms in
r-1
of
V_{q,\varpi}(r)
to cancel, we will need to make the following choices for the constants
q
and
\varpi
:

\displaystyle q=

\displaystyle\>\frac{(3-a^{2})m}{4a},

(6.20)

\displaystyle\varpi=

\displaystyle\>-\frac{1-a^{2}}{1+a^{2}}q=-\frac{(1-a^{2})(3-a^{2})m}{4a(1+a^{2})^{2}}.

(6.21)

The potentials
\widetilde{V}_{\rm stat}
and
\check{V}

We introduce the shorthand notation:

\Lambda_{+}=\Lambda_{+,m\ell}

and split
\widetilde{V}
as follows:

\widetilde{V}=\widetilde{V}_{\rm stat}+\check{V},

with

(1+a^{2})^{2}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}\widetilde{V}_{\rm stat}(r):=\sum_{k=2}^{4}\frac{1}{k!}\frac{d^{k}((r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}(1+a^{2})^{2}\widetilde{V_{0}})}{dr^{k}}\Big|_{r=1,\widetilde{\omega}=0,\Lambda=\Lambda_{+}}\cdot(r-1)^{k}+V_{1}(r)|_{a=1}

(6.22)

When
a=1
, the potential
V_{\rm stat}
may be interpreted as the potential corresponding to stationary solutions with respect to the vector field
K
(
\widetilde{\omega}=0
); see §
3.2
.

6.4.2.
Properties of the potentials

In this section, we will analyze the main quantitative and qualitative properties of the potentials introduced in §
6.4.1
.

The lemma below concerns precise quantitiative properties of the potentials in the form of their asymptotic behaviour in the limits
r\downarrow 1
and
r\to\infty
.

Lemma 6.5
.

The potentials defined in §
6.4.1
have the following asymptotic behaviour:

\displaystyle\widetilde{V}_{0}(r)=

\displaystyle\>(1+a^{2})^{-3}\left[-8am\widetilde{\omega}+2(\Lambda-m^{2})(1-a^{2})+(2m^{2}-\Lambda+2am\widetilde{\omega})(1-a^{2})^{2}\right](r-1)+

(6.23)

\displaystyle+\>(1+a^{2})^{-4}\Big[4(\Lambda-2m^{2})+6(3m^{2}-2\Lambda+4am\widetilde{\omega})(1-a^{2})

\displaystyle+(5\Lambda-10m^{2}-12am\widetilde{\omega})(1-a^{2})^{2}\Big](r-1)^{2}

\displaystyle+\Lambda O((r-1)^{3}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{V}_{0}(r)=

\displaystyle\>(1+a^{2})^{-3}\left[-8am\widetilde{\omega}-2m^{2}(1-a^{2})+2(m^{2}+am\widetilde{\omega})(1-a^{2})^{2}\right](r-1)

(6.24)

\displaystyle+\>(1+a^{2})^{-4}\left[-8m^{2}+2(9m^{2}+12ma\widetilde{\omega})(1-a^{2})-2(5m^{2}+6am\widetilde{\omega})(1-a^{2})^{2}\right](r-1)^{2}

\displaystyle+\>\Lambda(r^{2}+a^{2})^{-2}\Delta+(m^{2}+|m\widetilde{\omega}|)O((r-1)^{3}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{V}_{1}(r)=

\displaystyle\>(1+a^{2})^{-3}(1-a^{2})^{2}(r-1)+2(1+a^{2})^{-4}\left[3(1-a^{2})-4(1-a^{2})^{2}+(1-a^{2})^{3}\right](r-1)^{2}

(6.25)

\displaystyle+O((r-1)^{3}),

\displaystyle V_{q,\varpi}(r)=

\displaystyle\>\frac{\left(\Lambda-\frac{2a^{2}m^{2}}{(1+a^{2})}\right)\Delta}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}}-m\widetilde{\omega}(r-1)^{2}+(r-1)\Delta O(m^{2}+|m\widetilde{\omega}|),

(6.26)

\displaystyle\check{V}=

\displaystyle\>\left[-m\widetilde{\omega}+\frac{1}{4}(1-a^{2})(\Lambda-m^{2}+O(|m\widetilde{\omega}|))+(1-a^{2})^{2}O(|\Lambda|)\right](r-1)

(6.27)

\displaystyle+O(|m\widetilde{\omega}|+\Lambda(1-a^{2})+|\Lambda-\Lambda_{+}|)(r-1)^{2},

\displaystyle\widetilde{V}_{\rm stat}(r)=

\displaystyle\>\left[\frac{1}{4}(\Lambda_{+}-2m^{2})+\Lambda_{+}O((1-a^{2}))\right](r-1)^{2}+\Lambda_{+}O((r-1)^{3}),

(6.28)

\displaystyle V(r)=

\displaystyle\>\Lambda(r^{-2}+O_{1}(r^{-3})).

(6.29)

Proof.

To obtain (
6.23
)–(
6.27
), we perform a Taylor expansion in
r-1
.

Similarly, (
6.29
) follows by Taylor expanding in
\frac{1}{r}
at
r=\infty
. The term
O(r^{-3})
does not depend on
m,\omega,\Lambda
because we are using that
\frac{m^{2}+|m\omega|+1}{\Lambda}
is uniformly bounded, since
\Lambda\geq\max\{|m|(|m|+1),2|m\omega|\}
by (
2.4
) and (
2.5
).
∎

The following lemma concerns the qualitative behaviour of the potential
V_{0}
, via an investigation of the possible shapes of its graph. This lemma forms the analogue of
[
DRSR16
, Lemma 6.3.1]
and
[
DRSR16
, Lemma 6.3.2]
and includes a more refined analysis of the
a=1
case.

Lemma 6.6
.

The following properties hold:

(i)

The potential
V_{0}
is
either
:

(a)

strictly decreasing;

(b)

has a unique critical point
r^{0}_{\rm max}\geq 1
corresponding to a global maximum, with equality iff
a=1
and
m\omega=m\upomega_{+}=\frac{m^{2}}{2}
and
\Lambda\leq 2m^{2}
;

(c)

has exactly two critical points
1\leq r^{0}_{\rm min}<r^{0}_{\rm max}
, corresponding to a local minimum and local maximum, respectively, with equality iff
a=1
and
m\omega=m\upomega_{+}=\frac{m^{2}}{2}
and
\Lambda>2m^{2}
.

(ii)

There exists a numerical constant
B>0
(independent of any parameters), such that

1\leq r^{0}_{\rm max}<B.

(iii)

If we restrict to
m\omega<m\upomega_{+}
, then

\frac{dV_{0}}{dr}(1)=\frac{dV_{0}}{dr}(1)\geq 0,

so
V_{0}(r)
has exactly one critical point
r_{\max}^{0}
.

(a)

V_{0}
strictly decreasing (Case (i)(a)).

(b)

V_{0}
with one maximum and no minima (Case (i)(b)).

(c)

V_{0}
with one minimum away from
r=1
and one maximum (Case (i)(c)).

(d)

V_{0}
with one maximum at

r=1
and no minima (Case (i)(b)).

(e)

V_{0}
with one minimum at
r=1
and one maximum (Case (i)(c)).

Figure 2
.
The different shapes of the graph of
V_{0}(r)
corresponding to the cases outlined in Lemma
6.6
.

Proof.

We can write

\displaystyle(r^{2}+a^{2})^{3}\frac{dV_{0}}{dr}=

\displaystyle\>2(1+a^{2})am\omega(a^{2}-3r^{2})+4m^{2}a^{2}r+\Lambda[(3-2r)r^{2}-a^{4}+a^{2}(r-1)(3r+1)],

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dr}\left((r^{2}+a^{2})^{3}\frac{dV_{0}}{dr}\right)=

\displaystyle\>-12(1+a^{2})am\omega r-6\Lambda r(r-1)+a^{2}\Lambda(6r-2)+4a^{2}m^{2}.

Let
r_{1}<r_{2}
be the solutions of
\frac{d}{dr}\left((r^{2}+a^{2})^{3}\frac{dV_{0}}{dr}\right))(r_{i})=0
. Then

r_{1,2}=\frac{1}{2}(1+a^{2})(1-2ma\omega\Lambda^{-1})\mp\sqrt{\frac{1}{4}(1+a^{2})^{2}(2ma\omega\Lambda^{-1}-1)^{2}-\frac{a^{2}}{3}(1-2m^{2}\Lambda^{-1})}.

Suppose
m\omega>0
. Then clearly
r_{1}<1
if it is real. Suppose
m\omega\leq 0
. Then we can express:

r_{1}=\frac{1}{2}(1+a^{2})(1+2a|m\omega|\Lambda^{-1})-\sqrt{\frac{1}{4}(1+a^{2})^{2}(2a|m\omega|\Lambda^{-1}+1)^{2}-\frac{a^{2}}{3}(1-2m^{2}\Lambda^{-1})}

so, using that

\begin{split}\frac{1}{4}(1+a^{2})^{2}(2a|m\omega|\Lambda^{-1}+1)^{2}-\frac{a^{2}}{3}(1-2m^{2}\Lambda^{-1})\geq&\>\frac{1}{4}(1+a^{2})^{2}(2a|m\omega|\Lambda^{-1})^{2}+\frac{1}{4}(1+a^{2})^{2}-\frac{1}{3}a^{2}\\
\geq&\>\frac{1}{4}(1+a^{2})^{2}(2a|m\omega|\Lambda^{-1})^{2}\end{split}

we once again have that
r_{1}<\frac{1}{2}(1+a^{2})<1
.

In both cases,
(r^{2}+a^{2})^{3}\frac{dV_{0}}{dr}
can therefore have at most one critical point in
[1,\infty)
. We moreover have that

\lim_{r\to\infty}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{3}\frac{dV_{0}}{dr}=-\infty.

Hence,
\frac{dV_{0}}{dr}
either: A) strictly negative, B) vanishes at a unique point
r^{0}_{\rm max}
, C) vanishes at two points
r^{0}_{\rm min}<r^{0}_{\rm max}
. By the above observations together with the fact that
\lim_{r\to\infty}r^{2}V_{0}(r)=\Lambda
, we can moreover infer that in the case:
V_{0}(r)
is 1) strictly decreasing, 2)
V_{0}(r)
has a unique critical point
r^{0}_{\rm max}
which is a global maximum, or 3) has two critical points, a local minimum with agrees with
r^{0}_{\rm min}
and a local maximum which agrees with
r^{0}_{\rm max}
.

From (
6.23
) it follows that in the special case
a=1
and
\omega=\upomega_{+}
:
r=1
is either: A) a local minimum if
\Lambda>2m^{2}
, B) a global maximum if
\Lambda<2m^{2}
, C) a global maximum if
\Lambda=2m^{2}
.

Finally, we observe that there exists a numerical constant
A>0
, such that

|(r^{2}+a^{2})^{3}\frac{dV_{0}}{dr}+2\Lambda r^{3}-3(1+a^{2})(\Lambda-2am\omega)r^{2}|\leq A(\Lambda+|m\omega|+m^{2}r^{-1})r

Hence,

|\Lambda r^{0}_{\rm max}-(1+a^{2})(\Lambda-2am\omega)|\leq A(\Lambda+|m\omega|+m^{2}).

We divide the above inequality with
\Lambda
and use that
\Lambda\geq|m|(|m|+1)
and
\Lambda\geq 2|m\omega|
to conclude that there exists a numerical constant
B>0
such that
r^{0}_{\rm max}<B
.

It follows from (
6.23
) and (
6.25
) that when
m\widetilde{\omega}<0
:

\frac{dV_{0}}{dr}(1)=\frac{d\widetilde{V}_{0}}{dr}(1)\geq 0,

so it immediately follows that
V_{0}
cannot have a minimum.
∎

The lemma below concerns the quantitative behaviour of
V
around its extrema.

Lemma 6.7
.

The potential
V
has at most two critical points
r_{\min}<r_{\max}
, corresponding to a local minimum and a global maximum. Furthermore:

(i)

There exists a numerical constant
C>0
, such that

\displaystyle|r_{\max}-(1+\sqrt{2})|\leq

\displaystyle\>C(|m\omega|+m^{2})\Lambda^{-1}+C(1-a^{2}),

\displaystyle 0\leq r_{\min}-1\leq

\displaystyle\>C(|m\omega|+m^{2})\Lambda^{-1}+C(1-a^{2})\qquad\textnormal{if $m\omega\geq m\upomega_{+}$},

\displaystyle\textnormal{$V(r)$ has no minimum in $[1,\infty)$ if $m\omega<m\upomega_{+}$}.

(ii)

For sufficiently small
\frac{|m\omega|+m^{2}+1}{\Lambda}
and
\frac{\omega^{2}}{\Lambda}
, there exist constants
b>0
and
\delta>0
, such that

V(r)-\omega^{2}\geq b\Lambda\qquad\textnormal{for all $r\in(r_{\max}-\delta,r_{\max}+\delta)$}.

(iii)

For sufficiently small
\frac{|m\omega|+m^{2}+1}{\Lambda}
, there exist constants
b,B>0
, such that for all
r\geq 1
:

\displaystyle(r_{\max}-r)\frac{dV}{dr}(r)\geq

\displaystyle\>b\Lambda r^{-5}(r-1)(r-r_{\max})^{2}\qquad\textnormal{if $m\omega<m\upomega_{+}$},

\displaystyle(r_{\max}-r)\frac{dV}{dr}(r)\geq

\displaystyle\>b\Lambda r^{-5}(r-1)(r-r_{\max})^{2}-B\left(m\omega-m\upomega_{+}\right)r^{-4}(r-r_{\max})^{2}\qquad\textnormal{if $m\omega\geq m\upomega_{+}$}.

(iv)

Let
0<\eta<\frac{1}{4}
and
m\neq 0
. Consider
m\omega\leq m\upomega_{+}-\eta m^{2}
. Then there exists constants
b_{\eta},\delta_{\eta}>0
such that:

\displaystyle(r_{\max}-r)\frac{dV}{dr}(r)\geq

\displaystyle\>b_{\eta}r^{-4}(r-r_{\max})^{2}m^{2}\quad\textnormal{for $r\in[1,\infty)$},

\displaystyle V(r)-\omega^{2}\geq

\displaystyle\>b_{\eta}\Lambda\quad\textnormal{for $r\in(r_{\max}-\delta_{\eta},r_{\max}+\delta_{\eta})$},

with
r_{\rm max}-\delta_{\eta}>1
.

Proof.

First of all, since

\begin{split}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{3}\frac{dV_{0}}{dr}=&\>2(1+a^{2})am\omega(a^{2}-3r^{2})+4m^{2}a^{2}r+\Lambda[(3-2r)r^{2}-a^{4}+a^{2}(r-1)(3r+1)]\end{split}

and there exists a uniform constant
A>0
such that

(r^{2}+a^{2})^{3}\frac{dV_{1}}{dr}\leq Ar^{2},

it follows that there exists a numerical constant
C>0
, such that

\displaystyle|r^{0}_{\min}-r_{\min}|\leq C\frac{|m\omega|+m^{2}+1}{\Lambda},

\displaystyle|r^{0}_{\max}-r_{\max}|\leq C\frac{|m\omega|+m^{2}+1}{\Lambda}.

We can express
(r^{2}+a^{2})^{3}\frac{dV_{0}}{dr}
as follows by expanding around
a^{2}=1
:

\begin{split}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{3}\frac{dV_{0}}{dr}=&\>2\Lambda(r-1)(1-r^{2}+2r)-8am\widetilde{\omega}-2m(r-1)(m+3mr+6a\widetilde{\omega}(1+r))\\
&+\Lambda(1-a^{2})[3+r(3r-2)-(1-a^{2})]\\
&+\left[-4m^{2}r+6am\widetilde{\omega}(r^{2}-1)+2m^{2}\left(r^{2}-\frac{2}{3}\right)\right](1-a^{2})\\
&+2m(a\widetilde{\omega}+m)(1-a^{2})^{2}.\end{split}

(6.30)

We use that by Lemma
6.6
,
r_{\max}^{0}\leq B
, so we can estimate

(r_{\square}^{0}-1)((r_{\square}^{0})^{2}-2r_{\square}^{0}-1)\leq C(|m\omega|+m^{2})\Lambda^{-1}+C(1-a^{2}),

with
C>0
and
\square\in\{\min,\max\}
.

We conclude that

\displaystyle|r_{\max}^{0}-(1+\sqrt{2})|\leq

\displaystyle\>C(|m\omega|+m^{2})\Lambda^{-1}+C(1-a^{2}),

\displaystyle|r_{\min}^{0}-1|\leq

\displaystyle\>C(|m\omega|+m^{2})\Lambda^{-1}+C(1-a^{2}).

We conclude that (i) holds by applying in addition (iii) of Lemma
6.6
.

Then, for
1-a^{2}
suitably small, (ii) follows immediately from the above estimate for
r_{\max}
and continuity of
V(r)
, together with the smallness assumption on
\frac{|m\omega|+m^{2}+\omega^{2}+1}{\Lambda}
. For the remaining
a
, we refer to
[
DRSR16
, Lemma 8.3.1]
.
6
6

6

In the present paper, only the case of small
1-a^{2}
is relevant, but we include the remaining
a
for the sake of completeness.

We will now establish (iii). We will consider the case where
1-a^{2}
is suitably small. From (
6.30
), we obtain

\begin{split}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{3}\frac{dV_{0}}{dr}\geq&\>2\Lambda(r-1)(r_{\max}-r)(r+\sqrt{2}-1)-2\Lambda(r-1)(r_{\max}-(1+\sqrt{2}))(r+\sqrt{2}-1)\\
&-8am\widetilde{\omega}-2(r-1)(m^{2}+3m^{2}r+6am\widetilde{\omega}(1+r))+(1-a^{2})\left(4\Lambda-3\frac{2}{3}m^{2}\right)\end{split}

and hence, noting that
\Lambda\geq m^{2}
and applying (i),

\begin{split}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{3}r^{-1}(r_{\max}-r)\frac{dV_{0}}{dr}\geq&\>2\Lambda(r-1)(r_{\max}-r)^{2}-B(1+m^{2}+|m\omega|)|r_{\max}-r|(r-1)\\
&-8am\widetilde{\omega}r^{-1}(r_{\max}-r)\end{split}

For
r\in[1,1+\frac{3}{4}\sqrt{2}]
, we use that for
1-a^{2}
and
\frac{|m\omega|+m^{2}+1}{\Lambda}
suitably small,
r_{\max}>1+\frac{3}{4}\sqrt{2}
and there exists a constant
b>0
such that:

\begin{split}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{3}r^{-1}(r_{\max}-r)\frac{dV}{dr}\geq&\>b\Lambda(r-1)-8am\widetilde{\omega}r^{-1}(r_{\max}-r)\end{split}

Note that when
r\in[1+\frac{3}{4}\sqrt{2},\infty)
, we can estimate:

\frac{d}{dr}\left((r^{2}+a^{2})^{3}\frac{dV_{0}}{dr}\right)\leq-c\Lambda r^{2},

for some constant
c>0
, by using that the critical point
r_{2}
defined in the proof of Lemma
6.6
satisfies:

\left|r_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\left(1+a^{2}+\sqrt{1-\frac{a^{2}}{3}}\right)\right|\leq C\frac{|m\omega|+m^{2}+1}{\Lambda},

so
r_{2}<1+\frac{3}{4}\sqrt{2}
for
1-a
and
\frac{|m\omega|+m^{2}+1}{\Lambda}
sufficiently small. Since moreover

\frac{d}{dr}\left((r^{2}+1)^{3}\frac{dV_{1}}{dr}\right)\leq Cr,

we can integrate
\frac{d}{dr}\left((r^{2}+1)^{3}\frac{dV}{dr}\right)
from
r=r_{\rm max}
to conclude that for any
r\in[1+\frac{3}{4}\sqrt{2},\infty)
:

\begin{split}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{3}r^{-1}(r_{\max}-r)\frac{dV}{dr}\geq&\>b\Lambda(r_{\max}-r)^{2}.\end{split}

Combining the above lower bound estimates for
r^{5}(r_{\max}-r)\frac{dV}{dr}
then gives (iii) for
1-a
sufficiently small. The statement with remaining
a
follows in particular from
[
DRSR16
, Lemma 8.3.1]
.

We will now prove (iv). Consider first the case
m\omega>0
and
a=1
. Since
0<m\omega<m\upomega_{+}-\eta m^{2}=\frac{1-2\eta}{2}m^{2}
, we have that

r_{0}=\frac{m}{2\omega}=\frac{m^{2}}{2m\omega}>1.

Now consider the subcase
m\omega>\epsilon m^{2}
. Then we additionally have that
r_{0}<R
for some
R
depending on
\epsilon
.

We can write for
a=1
:

\begin{split}(r_{0}^{2}+1)^{2}(V_{0}(r_{0})-\omega^{2})=&\>4m\omega r_{0}-m^{2}+\Lambda(r_{0}-1)^{2}-\omega^{2}(r_{0}^{2}+1)^{2}\\
=&\>m^{2}+\Lambda\omega^{-2}\left(\frac{m}{2}-\omega\right)^{2}-\omega^{-2}\left(\frac{m^{2}}{4}+\omega^{2}\right)^{2}\\
=&\>\omega^{-2}\left(m^{2}\omega^{2}+\Lambda\widetilde{\omega}^{2}-(\widetilde{\omega}^{2}+\omega m)^{2}\right)\\
=&\>\widetilde{\omega}^{2}\omega^{-2}\left(\Lambda-\widetilde{\omega}^{2}-2\omega m\right)\\
=&\>\widetilde{\omega}^{2}\omega^{-2}\left(\Lambda-\omega^{2}-\frac{m^{2}}{4}-\omega m\right)\\
\geq&\>\widetilde{\omega}^{2}\omega^{-2}\left(\Lambda-(1-\eta^{2})m^{2}\right).\end{split}

By (
2.4
), we have that
\Lambda\geq|m|(|m|+1)
. Hence,

(V_{0}(r_{0})-\omega^{2})\geq c_{\epsilon,\eta}\Lambda.

Since
V_{1}\geq 0
, the above inequality holds also with
V_{0}
replaced by
V
.

Now consider the case
m\omega\leq\epsilon m^{2}
. Then we apply (
6.29
) to conclude that for
R_{0}>1
suitably large, there exists a
\epsilon>0
suitably small, such that

V(R_{0})-\omega^{2}\geq\frac{\Lambda}{2R_{0}^{2}}.

By combining the above and applying continuity of
V
in
a
, we can therefore conclude that there exists a
b_{\eta}>0
, such that for all
1-a\ll 1

V(r_{\rm max})-\omega^{2}\geq b_{\eta}\Lambda.

We now consider
\frac{dV}{dr}
. For
1-a\ll 1
, it follows from (
6.23
) that there exists a constant
c_{\eta}>0
such that
\frac{dV}{dr}(1)\geq c_{\eta}m^{2}>0
. Hence,
V
has exactly one critical point, which is the maximum
r_{\rm max}
. Furthermore,
r_{\rm max}-1\geq r_{0}-1=-(m\omega)^{-1}m\widetilde{\omega}\geq\frac{2\eta}{1-2\eta}+O(1-a^{2})>\frac{\eta}{2}
for
1-a
sufficiently small compared to
\eta
.

By taking
\delta_{\eta}>0
sufficiently small, we also have that
r_{\max}-\delta_{\eta}>1
, so after applying Taylor’s theorem around
r_{\rm max}
, there exists a constant
b_{\eta}>0
such that:

(r_{\max}-r)\frac{dV}{dr}(r)\geq b_{\eta}r^{-4}(r-r_{\max})^{2}m^{2},

for
r\in(r_{\max}-\delta_{\eta},r_{\rm max}+\delta_{\eta})
.

We now consider the case
m\omega<0
and
1-a\ll 1
. Suppose
\Lambda<Bm^{2}
for some arbitrarily large constant
B>0
. Then by (
6.23
)

\frac{dV}{dr}(1)=-8am\widetilde{\omega}+O(1-a^{2})(\Lambda+m^{2}+|m\widetilde{\omega}|)\geq\left(4+O(1-a^{2})\right)\Lambda.

We infer that
V
has a unique critical point
r_{\rm max}
which is a maximum. Furthermore, since there exists a constant
C>0
such that for
1\leq r\leq 2

\frac{d^{2}V}{dr^{2}}(r)\geq-C\Lambda,

we have that there exists a
\delta>0
such that
r_{\rm max}>1+\delta
. We moreover have that

(r_{\max}-r)\frac{dV}{dr}(r)\geq br^{-4}(r-r_{\max})^{2}m^{2},

for all
r\geq 1
. If
\Lambda\geq Bm^{2}
for
B>0
arbitrarily large, we conclude that (iv) holds.

If instead
\Lambda<Bm^{2}
, we can simply apply (iii) to conclude (iv).

In the case
a<a_{0}<1
, we refer to
[
DRSR16
, Lemma 8.3.1]
and
[
DRSR16
, Proposition 8.5.1]
for a proof of (iv).
∎

6.5.
Frequency-localized estimates

In this section, we consider smooth solutions
u:\mathbb{R}_{r_{*}}\to\mathbb{R}
to the boundary value problem:

\displaystyle u^{\prime\prime}+(\omega^{2}-V)u=

\displaystyle\>H,

(6.31)

\displaystyle[u^{\prime}+i\left(\omega-m\upomega_{+}\right)u](-\infty)=

\displaystyle\>0,

(6.32)

\displaystyle[u^{\prime}-i\omega u](+\infty)=

\displaystyle\>0,

(6.33)

where we used the notation
(\cdot)^{\prime}:=\frac{d}{dr_{*}}=\frac{\Delta}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\frac{d}{dr}
and
V(r)=V(r;a,m,\omega,\Lambda)
is the potential function satisfying (
6.17
)–(
6.19
), where we take the parameters
\omega,m,\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}
to denote
admissible frequencies
, according to the definition below.

Definition 6.2
.

We say that a frequency triple
(\omega,m,\Lambda)
, with
m\in\mathbb{Z}
,
|m|\leq m_{0}
, with
m_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
,
\Lambda,\omega\in\mathbb{R}
is
admissible
iff:

\Lambda\geq\max\{|m|(|m|+1),2|m\omega|\}

and
\Lambda(a,\omega,m,\ell)\to\Lambda_{+}|_{a=1}
as
|a|\uparrow 1
and
\omega\to\upomega_{+}
.

In the remainder of §
6.5
, we will restrict the consideration to the “near-extremal” setting, i.e. we will assume that
a_{0}\leq a\leq 1
, with
a_{0}
suitably close to 1.

6.5.1.
Frequency-localized currents

We wil derive integrated estimates by systematically integrating by parts via the use of
currents
. We define the following currents:

\displaystyle j_{1}^{h}[u]:=

\displaystyle\>h\Re(u^{\prime}\overline{u})-\frac{1}{2}h^{\prime}|u|^{2},

\displaystyle j_{2}^{y}[u]:=

\displaystyle\>y(|u^{\prime}|^{2}+(\omega^{2}-V)|u|^{2}),

\displaystyle j_{3}^{f}[u]:=

\displaystyle\>j_{1}^{f^{\prime}}[u]+j_{2}^{f}[u]=f(|u^{\prime}|^{2}+(\omega^{2}-V)|u|^{2})+f^{\prime}\Re(u^{\prime}\overline{u})-\frac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}|u|^{2},

\displaystyle j^{g_{\infty}}_{\infty}[u]:=

\displaystyle\>\frac{1}{2}g_{\infty}|u^{\prime}-i\omega u|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}g_{\infty}V|u|^{2},

\displaystyle j^{g_{+}}_{+}[u]:=

\displaystyle\>\frac{1}{2}g_{+}|u^{\prime}+i\widetilde{\omega}u|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}g_{+}\widetilde{V}|u|^{2},

\displaystyle j^{T}[u]:=

\displaystyle\>-\omega\Re(iu^{\prime}\overline{u}),

\displaystyle j^{K}[u]:=

\displaystyle\>-\widetilde{\omega}\Re(iu^{\prime}\overline{u}).

By applying (
6.31
), we obtain the following expressions for the derivatives of the above currents:

\displaystyle\left(j_{1}^{h}[u]\right)^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\>h(|u^{\prime}|^{2}+(V-\omega^{2})|u|^{2})-\frac{1}{2}h^{\prime\prime}|u|^{2}+h\Re(u\overline{H}),

\displaystyle\left(j_{2}^{y}[u]\right)^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\>y^{\prime}(|u^{\prime}|^{2}+(\omega^{2}-V)|u|^{2})-yV^{\prime}|u|^{2}+2y\Re(u^{\prime}\overline{H}),

\displaystyle\left(j_{3}^{f}[u]\right)^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\>2f^{\prime}|u^{\prime}|^{2}-fV^{\prime}|u|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime\prime}|u|^{2}+\Re(f^{\prime}u\overline{H}+2fu^{\prime}\overline{H}),

\displaystyle\left(j^{g_{\infty}}_{\infty}[u]\right)^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\>\frac{1}{2}g_{\infty}^{\prime}|u^{\prime}-i\omega u|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}(g_{\infty}V)^{\prime}|u|^{2}+\Re(g_{\infty}(u^{\prime}-i\omega u)\overline{H}),

\displaystyle\left(j^{g_{+}}_{+}[u]\right)^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\>\frac{1}{2}g_{+}^{\prime}|u^{\prime}+i\widetilde{\omega}u|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}(g_{+}\widetilde{V})^{\prime}|u|^{2}+\Re(g_{+}(u^{\prime}+i\widetilde{\omega}u)\overline{H}),

\displaystyle\left(j^{T}[u]\right)^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\>-\omega\Re(iH\overline{u}),

\displaystyle\left(j^{K}[u]\right)^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\>-\widetilde{\omega}\Re(iH\overline{u}).

It will be convenient to define an alternative to
j_{+}^{g_{+}}[u]
near
r=1
. For this purpose, we introduce the following rescaled variable

v:=e^{i(\widetilde{\omega}+\varpi)r_{*}}\Delta^{iq}u

Note that

\displaystyle u^{\prime}=(e^{-i(\widetilde{\omega}+\varpi)r_{*}}\Delta^{-iq}v)^{\prime}=

\displaystyle-i\left[\widetilde{\omega}+\varpi+q\frac{d\Delta}{dr}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{-1}\right]e^{-i(\widetilde{\omega}+\varpi)r_{*}}\Delta^{-iq}v

\displaystyle+e^{-i(\widetilde{\omega}+\varpi)r_{*}}\Delta^{-iq}v^{\prime},

\displaystyle e^{i(\widetilde{\omega}+\varpi)r_{*}}\Delta^{iq}u^{\prime\prime}=

\displaystyle\>v^{\prime\prime}-2i\left[(\widetilde{\omega}+\varpi)+q\frac{d\Delta}{dr}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{-1}\right]v^{\prime}

\displaystyle-\left[(\widetilde{\omega}+\varpi)^{2}+2q(\widetilde{\omega}+\varpi)\frac{d\Delta}{dr}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{-1}+q^{2}\left(\frac{d\Delta}{dr}\right)^{2}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{-2}\right]v

\displaystyle-iq\frac{\Delta}{(r^{2}+a^{2})}\left[\frac{d^{2}\Delta}{dr^{2}}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{-1}-2r\frac{d\Delta}{dr}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{-2}\right]v.

Recall from §
6.4.1
that

\begin{split}V_{q,\varpi}(r)=&\>\widetilde{V}(r)-\widetilde{\omega}^{2}+\left[(\widetilde{\omega}+\varpi)^{2}+2q(\widetilde{\omega}+\varpi)\frac{d\Delta}{dr}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{-1}+q^{2}\left(\frac{d\Delta}{dr}\right)^{2}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{-2}\right].\end{split}

Then (
6.31
) is equivalent to the following ODE:

\begin{split}v^{\prime\prime}&-2i\left[\widetilde{\omega}+\varpi+q\Delta\frac{d\Delta}{dr}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{-1}\right]v^{\prime}-V_{q,\varpi}v-iq\frac{\Delta}{(r^{2}+a^{2})}\left[\frac{d^{2}\Delta}{dr^{2}}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{-1}-2r\frac{d\Delta}{dr}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{-2}\right]v\\
=&\>e^{i(\widetilde{\omega}+\varpi)r_{*}}\Delta^{iq}H.\end{split}

(6.34)

We will make the following choices of
q,\varpi
:

\displaystyle q=

\displaystyle\>\frac{(3-a^{2})m}{4a},

\displaystyle\varpi=

\displaystyle\>-\frac{1-a^{2}}{1+a^{2}}q=-\frac{(1-a^{2})(3-a^{2})m}{4a(1+a^{2})^{2}}.

We now define the following current:

\displaystyle j_{++}^{g_{++}}[v]:=

\displaystyle\>\frac{1}{2}g_{++}|v^{\prime}|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}g_{++}V_{q,\varpi}|v|^{2},\qquad\textnormal{with}

\displaystyle\left(j_{++}^{g_{++}}[v]\right)^{\prime}:=

\displaystyle\>\frac{1}{2}g_{++}^{\prime}|v^{\prime}|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\left(g_{++}V_{q,\varpi}\right)^{\prime}|v|^{2}-\frac{\Delta g_{++}}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\left[\frac{d^{2}\Delta}{dr^{2}}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{-1}-2r\frac{d\Delta}{dr}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{-2}\right]\Re(iqv\frac{d\overline{v}}{dr})

\displaystyle+\Re(g_{++}v^{\prime}e^{-i(\widetilde{\omega}+\varpi)r_{*}}\Delta^{-iq}\overline{H}).

We will define the functions
h,y,f,g_{\infty},g_{+},g_{++}:\mathbb{R}_{r_{*}}\to\mathbb{R}
in §§
6.5.2
–
6.5.10
. We will occasionally abuse notation and interpret these functions as functions on
(1,\infty)_{r}
, implicitly interpreting
r_{*}
as a function of
r
.

6.5.2.
r
-weighted and
(r-1)
-weighted estimates

In this section, we derive estimates with
r
-weights in the region
r\gg 1
and
(r-1)
-weights in the region
r-1\ll 1
.

Proposition 6.8
.

Let
0<p<2
.

(i)

There exists a sufficiently large
R_{\infty}>1
and a constant
C>0
such that:

\int_{R_{\infty}}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2}p|u^{\prime}-i\omega u|^{2}+\frac{1}{4}(2-p)\Lambda r^{p-3}|u|^{2}\,dr\leq C\int_{R_{\infty}-1}^{R_{\infty}}\Lambda r^{p-2}|u|^{2}\,dr-\int_{\mathbb{R}}r^{p}\chi_{\infty}\Re((u^{\prime}-i\omega u)\overline{H})\,dr_{*}.

(6.35)

(ii)

There exists a
R_{+}>1
with
R_{+}-1
sufficiently small and a constant
b>0
such that:

b\int_{1}^{R_{+}}p\Delta^{-\frac{p}{2}-1}(r-1)|u^{\prime}+i\widetilde{\omega}u|^{2}+(2-p)\Lambda\Delta^{-\frac{p}{2}}(2(r-1)+1-a^{2})|u|^{2}\,dr\\
\leq\int_{1}^{R_{+}}m^{2}\Delta^{-\frac{p}{2}}(2(r-1)+1-a^{2})|u|^{2}\,dr+\int_{R_{+}}^{R_{+}+1}\Lambda r^{p-2}|u|^{2}\,dr\\
-\int_{1}^{R_{+}+1}\Delta^{-\frac{p}{2}}\chi_{+}\Re\left(\frac{dv}{dr}e^{-i(\widetilde{\omega}+\varpi)r_{*}}\Delta^{-iq}\overline{H}\right)\,dr.

(6.36)

(iii)

Let
\frac{m^{2}+|m\widetilde{\omega}|}{\Lambda}\ll 2-p
, then there exists a
R_{+}>1
with
R_{+}-1
sufficiently small and a constant
b>0
such that:

b\int_{1}^{R_{+}}p\Delta^{-\frac{p}{2}-1}(r-1)|u^{\prime}+i\widetilde{\omega}u|^{2}+(2-p)\Lambda\Delta^{-\frac{p}{2}}(2(r-1)+1-a^{2})|u|^{2}\,dr\\
\leq\int_{R_{+}}^{R_{+}+1}\Lambda r^{p-2}|u|^{2}\,dr-\int_{1}^{R_{+}+1}\Delta^{-\frac{p}{2}}\chi_{+}\Re\left(\frac{dv}{dr}e^{-i(\widetilde{\omega}+\varpi)r_{*}}\Delta^{-iq}\overline{H}\right)\,dr.

(6.37)

Proof.

Consider first the current
j^{g_{\infty}}_{\infty}
with
g_{\infty}(r)=r^{p}\chi_{\infty}(r)
, where
\chi
denotes a smooth cut-off function that satisfies:
\chi_{\infty}(r)=1
for
r\geq R_{\infty}
and
\chi_{\infty}(r)=0
for
r\leq R_{\infty}-1
, where we will take
R_{\infty}>2
suitably large. Integrating
(j^{g_{\infty}}_{\infty})^{\prime}
then gives the identity:

\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dr}(r^{p}\chi_{\infty})|u^{\prime}-i\omega u|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dr}(r^{p}V\chi_{\infty})|u|^{2}\,dr\\
=\frac{1}{2}\lim_{r_{*}\to\infty}\left[r^{p}|u^{\prime}-i\omega u|^{2}(r_{*})-\frac{1}{2}r^{p}V|u|^{2}\right]-\int_{\mathbb{R}}r^{p}\chi_{\infty}\Re((u^{\prime}-i\omega u)\overline{H})\,dr_{*}.

(6.38)

Since
(u^{\prime}-i\omega u)(\infty)=0
, we can appeal to the asymptotic expansion of
u^{\prime}-i\omega u
at
r=\infty
to conclude that

\lim_{r_{*}\to\infty}r^{q}|u^{\prime}-i\omega u|^{2}(r_{*})=0\quad\textnormal{for $q<4$}.

Since
\lim_{r\to\infty}r^{2}V=\Lambda
, the other limit appearing on the right-hand side of (
6.38
) has a good sign.

We furthermore apply (
6.29
) to conclude that:

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dr}(r^{p}V)\geq\frac{1}{4}(2-p)\Lambda r^{p-3}.

for
R_{\infty}
sufficiently large. Then (
6.35
) follows.

Consider now
j^{g_{++}}_{++}
with
g_{+}=-\Delta^{-\frac{p}{2}}\chi_{+}
, where
\chi_{+}
denotes a smooth cut-off function satisfying
\chi_{+}(r)=1
for
r\leq R_{+}
and
\chi_{+}(r)=0
for
r\geq R_{+}+1
, where we will take
R_{+}-1
suitably small.

Integrating
(j^{g_{++}}_{++})^{\prime}
results in the identity:

\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dr}(-\Delta^{-\frac{p}{2}}\chi_{+})|v^{\prime}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dr}(\Delta^{-\frac{p}{2}}V_{q,\varpi}\chi_{+})|v|^{2}\\
+\Delta^{-\frac{p}{2}}\chi_{+}\left[\frac{d^{2}\Delta}{dr^{2}}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{-1}-2r\frac{d\Delta}{dr}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{-2}\right]\Re(iqv\frac{d\overline{v}}{dr})\,dr\\
=\frac{1}{2}\lim_{r_{*}\to-\infty}\left[\Delta^{-\frac{p}{2}}|v^{\prime}|^{2}(r_{*})-\frac{1}{2}\Delta^{-\frac{p}{2}}V_{q,\varpi}|v|^{2}(r_{*})\right]+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Delta^{-\frac{p}{2}}\chi_{+}\Re(v^{\prime}e^{-i(\widetilde{\omega}+\varpi)r_{*}}\Delta^{-iq}\overline{H})\,dr_{*},

(6.39)

with

\displaystyle q=

\displaystyle\>\frac{(3-a^{2})m}{4a},

\displaystyle\varpi=

\displaystyle-\frac{(1-a^{2})(3-a^{2})m}{4a(1+a^{2})^{2}}.

By the boundary condition on
u
at
r=-\infty
and (
6.26
), the limit on the right-hand side of (
6.39
) is equal to zero for
p<2
. Furthermore, there exists a constant
b>0
such that

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dr}(\Delta^{-\frac{p}{2}}V_{q,\varpi})\geq\frac{1}{8}(2-p)\Lambda\left(1-\frac{m^{2}}{\Lambda}-4\frac{m\widetilde{\omega}}{\Lambda}\right)\frac{d\Delta}{dr}\Delta^{-\frac{p}{2}}.

If we assume that
\Lambda>(1+\epsilon)m^{2}
for some
\epsilon>0
and that
R_{+}-1
is suitably small, and, in the case
m\widetilde{\omega}>0
, we assume additionally that
\frac{m\widetilde{\omega}}{\Lambda}\ll 1
, we in fact obtain:

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dr}(\Delta^{-\frac{p}{2}}V_{q,\varpi})\geq b(2-p)\Lambda\frac{d\Delta}{dr}\Delta^{-\frac{p}{2}}.

(6.40)

We apply Young’s inequality to estimate

\left|\Delta^{-\frac{p}{2}}\left[\frac{d^{2}\Delta}{dr^{2}}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{-1}-2r\frac{d\Delta}{dr}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{-2}\right]\Re(iqv\frac{d\overline{v}}{dr})\right|\\
\leq 2r\Delta^{-\frac{p}{2}}(1-2(r-1)-(1-a^{2}))\frac{|m|(1-2(1-a)+O(1-a^{2}))}{2}|v|\left|\frac{dv}{dr}\right|\\
\leq r\Delta^{-\frac{p}{2}}|m||v|\left|\frac{dv}{dr}\right|\\
\leq\frac{p}{8}\Delta^{-\frac{p}{2}-1}\frac{d\Delta}{dr}|v^{\prime}|^{2}+\frac{8m^{2}r^{2}\Delta}{p(\frac{d\Delta}{dr})^{2}}\Delta^{-\frac{p}{2}}\frac{d\Delta}{dr}|v|^{2}

and note that we can bound uniformly in
a\leq 1
:
\frac{\Delta}{(\frac{d\Delta}{dr})^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{4}
. We then obtain:

b\int_{1}^{R_{+}}p\Delta^{-\frac{p}{2}-1}(2(r-1)+1-a^{2})|v^{\prime}|^{2}+(2-p)\Lambda\Delta^{-\frac{p}{2}}(2(r-1)+1-a^{2})|v|^{2}\,dr\\
\leq\int_{1}^{R_{+}}m^{2}\Delta^{-\frac{p}{2}}(2(r-1)+1-a^{2})|v|^{2}\,dr+\int_{R_{+}}^{R_{+}+1}\Lambda r^{p-2}|v|^{2}\,dr-\int_{1}^{R_{+}+1}\Delta^{-\frac{p}{2}}\chi\Re\left(\frac{dv}{dr}e^{-i(\widetilde{\omega}+\varpi)r_{*}}\Delta^{-iq}\overline{H}\right)\,dr.

We conclude (
6.36
) by noting that
|v|=|u|
and

\Delta^{-\frac{p}{2}-1}(r-1)|v^{\prime}|^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}\Delta^{-\frac{p}{2}-1}(r-1)|u^{\prime}+i\widetilde{\omega}u|^{2}-C(1-a)^{2}m^{2}\Delta^{-\frac{p}{2}-1}(r-1)|u|^{2}-Cm^{2}\Delta^{-\frac{p}{2}-1}(r-1)^{3}|u|^{2}\\
\geq\frac{1}{2}\Delta^{-\frac{p}{2}-1}(r-1)|u^{\prime}+i\widetilde{\omega}u|^{2}-Cm^{2}\Delta^{-\frac{p}{2}}(2(r-1)+(1-a^{2}))|u|^{2}

Finally, (
6.37
) follows from (
6.36
) by absorbing the integral over
[1,R_{+}]
on the right-hand side, using that
m^{2}\Lambda^{-1}\ll 2-p
and applying (
6.40
).

∎

6.5.3.
Partitioning the frequency ranges

We will split up the ranges of the frequencies
(\omega,\Lambda,m)
, in order to separate different difficulties that show up when trying to establish integrated energy estimates.

We consider
a\geq a_{0}
, with
a_{0}
suitably large. Let
\alpha,\epsilon_{\rm width},\omega_{\rm high},\eta_{\flat}>0
be constants. We will choose
\eta_{\flat}
suitably small,
\epsilon_{\rm width}<\alpha^{2}\ll\eta_{\flat}^{2}
and
\omega_{\rm high}^{2}\gg\epsilon_{\rm width}^{-1}
. Then we define:

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{\sharp}_{\rm sr}:=

\displaystyle\>\bigg\{(\omega,m,\Lambda)\>\textnormal{admissible}\,\big|\,\Lambda\geq\left(\frac{a}{1+a}+\alpha\right)^{-2}\omega^{2}_{\rm high},\>m\omega\in\left(0,m\upomega_{+}+\alpha\sqrt{\Lambda m^{2}}\right),\>\textnormal{and:}

\displaystyle\Lambda\geq\left(\frac{a}{1+a}+\alpha\right)^{-2}\omega^{2}_{\rm high}m^{2}\,\textnormal{or}\,\left|m\widetilde{\omega}\right|\geq\eta_{\flat}m^{2}\bigg\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{\sharp,\omega}_{\rm nsr}:=

\displaystyle\>\left\{(\omega,m,\Lambda)\>\textnormal{admissible}\,\big|\,|\omega|\geq\omega_{\rm high},\>\Lambda<\epsilon_{\rm width}\omega^{2},\>m\omega\notin\left(0,m\upomega_{+}+\alpha\sqrt{\Lambda m^{2}}\right)\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{\sharp,\Lambda}_{\rm nsr}:=

\displaystyle\>\left\{(\omega,m,\Lambda)\>\textnormal{admissible}\,\big|\,\Lambda\geq\epsilon^{-1}_{\rm width}\omega_{\rm high}^{2},\>\Lambda>\epsilon_{\rm width}^{-1}\omega^{2},\>m\omega\notin\left(0,m\upomega_{+}+\alpha\sqrt{\Lambda m^{2}}\right)\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{\sharp,\sim}_{\rm nsr}:=

\displaystyle\>\bigg\{(\omega,m,\Lambda)\>\textnormal{admissible}\,\big|\,|\omega|\geq\omega_{\rm high},\>\epsilon_{\rm width}\Lambda\leq\omega^{2}\leq\epsilon^{-1}_{\rm width}\Lambda,\>m\omega\notin\left(0,m\upomega_{+}+\alpha\sqrt{\Lambda m^{2}}\right)\bigg\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{\flat}:=

\displaystyle\>\left\{(\omega,m,\Lambda)\>\textnormal{admissible}\,\big|\,|\omega|<\omega_{\rm high}|m|,\>\Lambda\leq\epsilon_{\rm width}^{-1}\omega^{2}_{\rm high}m^{2}\right\}\setminus(\mathcal{G}^{\sharp}_{\rm sr}\cup\mathcal{G}^{\sharp,\sim}_{\rm nsr}).

It is straightforward to verify that, given the assumptions on
\alpha,\epsilon_{\rm width},\omega_{\rm high}
, the frequency ranges
\mathcal{G}^{\sharp}_{\rm sr},\mathcal{G}^{\sharp,\Lambda}_{\rm nsr},\mathcal{G}^{\sharp,\sim}_{\rm nsr}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\flat}
partition the full set of admissible frequencies; see
[
DRSR16
, Lemma 8.1.1]
proving this in the case of a similar partition.

We will split
\mathcal{G}^{\flat}
further into two parts:

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{\flat}_{<}:=

\displaystyle\>\left\{(\omega,m,\Lambda)\in\mathcal{G}^{\flat}\,\big|\,|\omega|\leq\omega_{\rm high}|m|,\>\Lambda\leq\epsilon_{\rm width}^{-1}\omega^{2}_{\rm high}m^{2},\>\left|m\widetilde{\omega}\right|<\eta_{\flat}m^{2}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{\flat}_{>}:=

\displaystyle\>\left\{(\omega,m,\Lambda)\in\mathcal{G}^{\flat}\,\big|\,|\omega|\leq\omega_{\rm high},\>\Lambda\leq\epsilon_{\rm width}^{-1}\omega^{2}_{\rm high},\>\left|m\widetilde{\omega}\right|\geq\eta_{\flat}m^{2}\right\}.

Observe that
\mathcal{G}^{\flat}_{>}
is a bounded frequency range and
\mathcal{G}^{\flat}_{<}
is only a bounded frequency range if
|m|
is bounded.

We will exclude the case
m=0
, as that has already been treated in
[
Are12
]
. Note the following points:

•

In §
6.5.4
–§
6.5.7
,
we will not make any further (boundedness) assumptions on
m
.

•

The estimates in §§
6.5.8
–
6.5.9
will only be useful with the additional assumption of boundedness of
m
. That is to say,
we will not keep careful track of the dependence
m
-dependence of constants in the estimates derived in those sections
.

We will employ the following notation to indicate integration and summation over the bounded frequency ranges at fixed
m
:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{B}_{<}}\sum_{\ell}(\cdot)\,d\widetilde{\omega}=:

\displaystyle\>\int_{\{|\omega|\leq\omega_{\rm high}|m|\}\cap\{\left|m\widetilde{\omega}\right|<\eta_{\flat}m^{2}\}}\sum_{\{\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{|m|}\,|\,\Lambda\leq\epsilon_{\rm width}^{-1}\omega^{2}_{\rm high}m^{2}\}}(\cdot)\,d\widetilde{\omega},

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{B}_{>}}\sum_{\ell}(\cdot)\,d\widetilde{\omega}=:

\displaystyle\>\int_{\{|\omega|\leq\omega_{\rm high}|m|\}\cap\{\left|m\widetilde{\omega}\right|\geq\eta_{\flat}m^{2}\}}\sum_{\{\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{|m|}\,|\,\Lambda\leq\epsilon_{\rm width}^{-1}\omega^{2}_{\rm high}m^{2}\}}(\cdot)\,d\widetilde{\omega}.

6.5.4.
\mathcal{G}^{\sharp}_{\rm sr}
: superradiant,
\Lambda
-dominated frequencies

In this section, we deal with the case of superradiant frequencies for which either
\Lambda\gg m^{2}
or
m\omega
is bounded away from the superradiant threshold frequency
m\upomega_{+}
.

Proposition 6.9
.

Let
(\omega,m,\Lambda)\in\mathcal{G}^{\sharp}_{\rm sr}
. For
a\leq 1
with
1-a
suitably small,
\epsilon>0
and
E,\omega_{\rm high}
suitably large, there exists a constant
c>0
such that:

(i)

For
\Lambda>\left(\frac{a}{1+a}+\alpha\right)\omega_{\rm high}^{2}m^{2}
:

c\int_{R_{+}}^{R_{\infty}}|u^{\prime}|^{2}+(\Lambda+\widetilde{\omega}^{2})|u|^{2}\,dr\\
\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Re((f^{\prime}+h)u\overline{H}+2fu^{\prime}\overline{H})+(r^{2}-a^{2})^{-1}\Delta^{\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\chi_{+}\Re\left(-\frac{dv}{dr}e^{-i(\widetilde{\omega}+\varpi)r_{*}}\Delta^{-iq}\overline{H}\right)\,dr_{*}\\
+E\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\chi_{K}\widetilde{\omega}+\chi_{T}\omega)\Re(iu\overline{H})\,dr_{*},

(6.41)

where
f,h,\chi_{K},\chi_{T}
are defined in the proof below and
\chi_{+}
is defined in the proof of Proposition
6.8
.

(ii)

For
\Lambda\leq\left(\frac{a}{1+a}+\alpha\right)\omega_{\rm high}^{2}m^{2}
and
|m\widetilde{\omega}|\geq\eta_{\flat}m^{2}
:

c\int_{1}^{R_{\infty}}|u^{\prime}|^{2}+(\Lambda+\widetilde{\omega}^{2})|u|^{2}\,dr\\
\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Re((f^{\prime}+h)u\overline{H}+2fu^{\prime}\overline{H})+E\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\chi_{K}\widetilde{\omega}+\chi_{T}\omega)\Re(iu\overline{H})\,dr_{*},

(6.42)

with the functions
f,h,\chi_{K},\chi_{T}
defined in the proof below.

Proof.

The proof consists of
three steps
which each feature a different type of current.

Step 1
: a
j^{f}_{3}
-current

For
\omega_{\rm high}
suitably large, properties (i) and (iii) of Lemma
6.7
are satisfied for frequencies
(\omega,m,\Lambda)\in\mathcal{G}^{\sharp}_{\rm sr}
with
\Lambda>\left(\frac{a}{1+a}+\alpha\right)\omega_{\rm high}^{2}m^{2}
, which implies: for
\omega_{\rm high}
suitably large and
\alpha
suitably small, there exists constants
b,B>0
such that

\displaystyle(r_{\rm max}-r)\frac{dV}{dr}(r)\geq

\displaystyle\>b\Lambda r^{-5}(r-1)(r-r_{\rm max})^{2}\quad\textnormal{if $m\widetilde{\omega}\leq 0$},

\displaystyle(r_{\rm max}-r)\frac{dV}{dr}(r)\geq

\displaystyle\>b\Lambda r^{-5}(r-1)(r-r_{\rm max})^{2}-Bm\widetilde{\omega}r^{-4}(r-r_{\rm max})^{2}\quad\textnormal{if $m\widetilde{\omega}>0$}

with
r_{\rm max}
arbitrarily close to
1+\sqrt{2}
, given
\omega_{\rm high}
suitably large and
\alpha
suitably small. We will refer to the condition
\Lambda>\left(\frac{a}{1+a}+\alpha\right)\omega_{\rm high}^{2}m^{2}
as
Condition A
.

We refer to Figure 1(B) for the shape of the potential when
m\widetilde{\omega}<0
(as
m\widetilde{\omega}\uparrow 0
, the shape approaches Figure 1(E)) and Figure 1(C) when
m\widetilde{\omega}>0
(approaching the Figure 1(E) as
m\widetilde{\omega}\downarrow 0
).

If
(\omega,m,\Lambda)\in\mathcal{G}^{\sharp}_{\rm sr}
with
|m\widetilde{\omega}|\geq\eta_{\flat}m^{2}
and
\Lambda\leq\left(\frac{a}{1+a}+\alpha\right)\omega_{\rm high}^{2}m^{2}
, we use that
\alpha\ll\eta_{\flat}
to infer that
m\widetilde{\omega}<0
, so property (iv) of Lemma
6.7
is satisfied. Hence,

(r_{\max}-r)\frac{dV}{dr}(r)\geq b_{\eta_{\flat}}r^{-4}(r-r_{\max})^{2}\Lambda,

with
1<r_{\max}=r_{\max}(\eta_{\flat})<B
for some suitably large constant
B>0
. We will refer to the condition
|m\widetilde{\omega}|\geq\eta_{\flat}m^{2}
and
\Lambda\leq\left(\frac{a}{1+a}+\alpha\right)\omega_{\rm high}^{2}m^{2}
as
Condition B
.

We will construct a function
f
that is adapted to
r_{\rm max}
, but does not take into account the possible presence of
r_{\rm min}
in the
m\widetilde{\omega}>0
case given Condition A.

Consider Condition A with
m\widetilde{\omega}<0
or Condition B. We can then construct an appropriate
f:(1,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
as follows: let
R_{\infty}>3
be arbitrarily large and define
c_{0}=r_{0}^{-1}\frac{\arctan(r_{*}(r_{1})-r_{*}(r_{\rm max}))}{\arctan(r_{*}(r_{\rm max})-r_{*}(r_{0}))}
, then
c_{0}>0
and we require

f(r):=\begin{cases}-r^{-1}&1\leq r\leq 1+\frac{1}{4}(r_{\rm max}-1)=:r_{0}\\
\frac{1}{2}r_{0}^{-1}\frac{\arctan(r_{*}(r)-r_{*}(r_{\rm max}))}{\arctan(r_{*}(r_{\rm max})-r_{*}(r_{0}))}&1+\frac{1}{2}(r_{\rm max}-1)\leq r\leq 1+2(r_{\rm max}-1)=:r_{1}\\
\frac{3}{4}c_{0}+\frac{c_{0}}{8}\frac{r-r_{2}}{R_{\infty}-r_{2}}&r_{2}:=1+3(r_{\rm max}-1)<r\leq R_{\infty}\\
c_{0}&r>R_{\infty}+1\end{cases}

It is possible to construct a smooth
f
satisfying the above properties with moreover
\frac{df}{dr}>0
for
r\leq R_{\infty}
via a standard mollification argument.

In the case of Condition A with
m\widetilde{\omega}>0
, we require instead

f(r):=\begin{cases}(r-1)&1\leq r\leq 1+\frac{1}{2}\delta,\\
-r^{-1}&1+\delta\leq r\leq 1+\frac{1}{4}(r_{\rm max}-1)=:r_{0}\\
\frac{1}{2}r_{0}^{-1}\frac{\arctan(r_{*}(r)-r_{*}(r_{\rm max}))}{\arctan(r_{*}(r_{\rm max})-r_{*}(r_{0}))}&1+\frac{1}{2}(r_{\rm max}-1)\leq r\leq 1+2(r_{\rm max}-1)=:r_{1}\\
\frac{3}{4}c_{0}+\frac{1}{8}c_{0}\frac{r-r_{2}}{R_{\infty}-r_{2}}&r_{2}:=1+3(r_{\rm max}-1)<r\leq R_{\infty}\\
c_{0}&r>R_{\infty}+1.\end{cases}

Note that we do not have that
f^{\prime}>0
in
1\leq r\leq 1+\delta
. We do have that
|\frac{df}{dr}|\leq\frac{1}{2}B\delta^{-1}
for some constant
B>0
.

We now integrate
(j^{f}_{3}[u])^{\prime}
to obtain:

\int_{\mathbb{R}}2f^{\prime}|u^{\prime}|^{2}-fV^{\prime}|u|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime\prime}|u|^{2}\,dr_{*}=c_{0}(|u^{\prime}|^{2}+\omega^{2}|u|^{2})(\infty)+(|u^{\prime}|^{2}+\widetilde{\omega}^{2}|u|^{2})(-\infty)\\
+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Re(f^{\prime}u\overline{H}+2fu^{\prime}\overline{H})\,dr_{*}.

(6.43)

Furthermore, there exist constants
c,C>0
such that under Condition A

\displaystyle-f\frac{dV}{dr}(r)\geq

\displaystyle\>c\Lambda r^{-5}(r-1)(r-r_{\rm max})^{2}\quad\textnormal{if $m\widetilde{\omega}\leq 0$},

\displaystyle-f\frac{dV}{dr}(r)\geq

\displaystyle\>c\Lambda r^{-5}(r-1)(r-r_{\rm max})^{2}-C\frac{m\widetilde{\omega}}{\Lambda}\Lambda r^{-4}(r-r_{\rm max})^{2}\quad\textnormal{if $m\widetilde{\omega}>0$}.

We moreover have that
f^{\prime\prime\prime}<0
in
(r_{\rm max}-\delta,r_{\rm max}+\delta)
, for
\delta>0
suitably small and
f^{\prime\prime\prime}
is dominated by
-fV^{\prime}
when
r\notin(r_{\rm max}-\delta,r_{\rm max}+\delta)
, since
\Lambda\gg 1
. Note finally, that in the case
m\widetilde{\omega}>0
,
\frac{m\widetilde{\omega}}{\Lambda}\leq\frac{m\widetilde{\omega}}{\sqrt{m^{2}\Lambda}}<\alpha
can be made arbitrarily small by taking
\alpha
suitably small. Hence, given
R_{+}
as in Proposition
6.8
, there exists
\alpha
suitably small such that:

\displaystyle-f\frac{dV}{dr}(r)\geq

\displaystyle\>c\Lambda r^{-5}(r-1)(r-r_{\rm max})^{2}

if
m\widetilde{\omega}>0
and
r\geq R_{+}

\displaystyle\left|f\frac{dV}{dr}(r)\right|\leq

\displaystyle\>C\alpha\Lambda

\displaystyle\textnormal{if $m\widetilde{\omega}>0$ and $1\leq r\leq R_{+}$}.

Under Condition B, we obtain

-f\frac{dV}{dr}(r)\geq c\Lambda r^{-4}(r-r_{\rm max})^{2}.

As in the case of Condition A,
f^{\prime\prime\prime}<0
in
(r_{\rm max}-\delta,r_{\rm max}+\delta)
, for
\delta>0
suitably small and
f^{\prime\prime\prime}
is dominated by
-fV^{\prime}
when
r\notin(r_{\rm max}-\delta,r_{\rm max}+\delta)
.

Step 2
: a
j^{h}_{1}
-current

By (ii) and (iv) of Lemma
6.7
, for either Condition A or B, there exists a
\delta>0
and
b>0
suitably small, such that

V(r)-\omega^{2}\geq b\Lambda\quad\textnormal{for all $r\in(r_{\rm max}-\delta,r_{\rm max}+\delta)$.}

We now let
h:(1,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
be a smooth function, such that:

h(r)=\begin{cases}A&(r_{\rm max}-\frac{\delta}{2},r_{\rm max}+\frac{\delta}{2})\\
0&r\notin(r_{\rm max}-\delta,r_{\rm max}+\delta),\end{cases}

with
A>0
a constant that will be chosen suitably below.
By adding
(j_{1}^{h}[u])^{\prime}
to the integral of
(j_{3}^{f}[u])^{\prime}
, we obtain:

\int_{\mathbb{R}}(2f^{\prime}+h)|u^{\prime}|^{2}-fV^{\prime}|u|^{2}+h(V-\omega^{2})|u|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime\prime}|u|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}h^{\prime\prime}|u|^{2}\,dr_{*}=c_{0}(|u^{\prime}|^{2}+\omega^{2}|u|^{2})(\infty)\\
+(|u^{\prime}|^{2}+\widetilde{\omega}^{2}|u|^{2})(-\infty)+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Re((f^{\prime}+h)u\overline{H}+2fu^{\prime}\overline{H})\,dr_{*}.

Let
R_{+}>1
. By choosing
\omega_{\rm high}
suitably large compared to
A
and
\alpha
suitably small compared to
R_{+}-1
, the left-hand side above is non-negative negative for
r\in[R_{+},\infty)
and we obtain particular under Condition A:

c\int_{R_{+}}^{R_{\infty}}|u^{\prime}|^{2}+\Lambda|u|^{2}\,dr+A\int_{r_{\rm max}-\frac{\delta}{2}}^{r_{\rm max}+\frac{\delta}{2}}\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta}(|u^{\prime}|^{2}+b\Lambda|u|^{2})\,dr\\
\leq(|u^{\prime}|^{2}+\omega^{2}|u|^{2})(\infty)+(|u^{\prime}|^{2}+\widetilde{\omega}^{2}|u|^{2})(-\infty)\\
-C(\alpha+\delta)\int_{1}^{1+\delta}\Lambda(r-1)|u|^{2}+B\delta^{-1}\int_{1+\frac{\delta}{2}}^{1+\delta}|u^{\prime}|^{2}\,dr+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Re((f^{\prime}+h)u\overline{H}+2fu^{\prime}\overline{H})\,dr_{*}.

(6.44)

Note moreover that we can further estimate

\begin{split}B\delta^{-1}\int_{1+\frac{\delta}{2}}^{1+\delta}|u^{\prime}|^{2}\,dr\leq&\>2B\int_{1+\frac{\delta}{2}}^{1+\delta}\delta^{-1}|u^{\prime}+i\widetilde{\omega}u|^{2}+\delta^{-1}\widetilde{\omega}^{2}|u|^{2}\,dr\\
\leq&\>2B\int_{1+\frac{\delta}{2}}^{1+\delta}\delta^{1-\epsilon}(r-1)^{-2+\epsilon}|u^{\prime}+i\widetilde{\omega}u|^{2}+\alpha^{2}\left(\frac{\delta}{2}\right)^{-1-\epsilon}(r-1)^{\epsilon}\Lambda|u|^{2}\,dr.\end{split}

(6.45)

Under Condition B, we obtain instead

c\int_{1}^{R_{\infty}}|u^{\prime}|^{2}+\Lambda|u|^{2}\,dr\leq(|u^{\prime}|^{2}+\omega^{2}|u|^{2})(\infty)+(|u^{\prime}|^{2}+\widetilde{\omega}^{2}|u|^{2})(-\infty)+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Re((f^{\prime}+h)u\overline{H}+2fu^{\prime}\overline{H})\,dr_{*}.

(6.46)

We further estimate the right-hand side of (
6.44
), using (
6.45
), by combining it with (
6.37
) with
p=1-\epsilon
and taking
\alpha\ll\delta
suitably small to conclude:

c\int_{R_{+}}^{R_{\infty}}|u^{\prime}|^{2}+\Lambda|u|^{2}\,dr+A\int_{r_{\rm max}-\frac{\delta}{2}}^{r_{\rm max}+\frac{\delta}{2}}\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta}(|u^{\prime}|^{2}+b\Lambda|u|^{2})\,dr\\
\leq(|u^{\prime}|^{2}+\omega^{2}|u|^{2})(\infty)+(|u^{\prime}|^{2}+\widetilde{\omega}^{2}|u|^{2})(-\infty)\\
+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Re((f^{\prime}+h)u\overline{H}+2fu^{\prime}\overline{H})-(r^{2}-a^{2})^{-1}\Delta^{\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\chi_{+}\Re\left(\frac{dv}{dr}\overline{H}\right)\,dr_{*}.

(6.47)

Note finally that we can add
\widetilde{\omega}^{2}|u|^{2}
inside the integral on the left-hand side, since
\widetilde{\omega}^{2}\leq\alpha^{2}\Lambda
.

Step 3
:
j^{K}
- and
j^{T}
-currents

In the final step of the proof, we estimate the boundary terms at
r=-\infty
and
r=\infty
appearing on the right-hand side of (
6.46
) and (
6.47
). For
m\widetilde{\omega}>0
this immediately follows by integrating
(j^{K}[u])^{\prime}=0
and
(j^{T}[u])^{\prime}=0
. For
m\widetilde{\omega}<0
we need to modify the currents.

First, we consider
(E\chi_{K}j^{K}[u])^{\prime}
, where
E\geq 2c_{0}
is a constant and
\chi_{K}
denotes a smooth cut-off function such that

\chi_{K}(r)=\begin{cases}1&r\in[1,r_{\rm max}-\frac{\delta}{2}]\\
0&r\in[r_{\rm max}+\frac{\delta}{2},\infty),\end{cases}

with
\left|\frac{d\chi}{dr}\right|\leq B\delta^{-1}
for some
B>0
independent of
\delta
.
Then we can express:
